On the Piatetski-Shapiro construction for integral models of Shimura varieties Thanks: J.S. was partially supported by funding from the European Research Council (ERC) under the European Union’s Horizon 2020 research and innovation program (grant agreement No. 884596).
Pol van Hoften and Jack Sempliner

Abstract
. We study the Piatetski-Shapiro construction, which takes a totally real field 𝖥 \mathsf{F} and a Shimura datum ( 𝖦 , 𝖷 ) {(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})} and produces a new Shimura datum ( 𝖧 , 𝖸 ) {(\mathsf{H},\mathsf{Y})} . If 𝖥 \mathsf{F} is Galois, then the Galois group Γ \Gamma of 𝖥 \mathsf{F} acts on ( 𝖧 , 𝖸 ) {(\mathsf{H},\mathsf{Y})} , and we show that the Γ \Gamma -fixed points of the Shimura varieties for ( 𝖧 , 𝖸 ) {(\mathsf{H},\mathsf{Y})} recover the Shimura varieties for ( 𝖦 , 𝖷 ) {(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})} under some hypotheses. For Shimura varieties of Hodge type with parahoric level, we show that the same is true for the p p -adic integral models constructed by Pappas–Rapoport. Along the way, we study the Γ \Gamma -fixed points of the Igusa stacks of [ 38 , 3 ] for ( 𝖧 , 𝖸 ) {(\mathsf{H},\mathsf{Y})} and prove optimal results.



1.
Introduction

Let
{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}
be a Shimura datum of Hodge type and let
\mathsf{F}
be a Galois totally real field with Galois group
\Gamma=\text{Gal}(\mathsf{F}/\mathbb{Q})
. Define
\mathsf{H}:=\operatorname{Res}_{\mathsf{F}/\mathbb{Q}}\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{F}}
and let
\mathsf{Y}
be the Shimura datum for
\mathsf{H}
induced by
\mathsf{X}
; we call
{(\mathsf{H},\mathsf{Y})}
the
Piatetski-Shapiro construction
for
{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}
associated to
\mathsf{F}
. This is
not
of Hodge type if
[\mathsf{F}:\mathbb{Q}]>1
, but there is a modified Shimura datum
{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}\xhookrightarrow{}{(\mathsf{H},\mathsf{Y})}
that is of Hodge type, see Section
3.6
. Then
\Gamma
acts on
{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}
and there is a morphism
{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}\to{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}
that is
\Gamma
-equivariant. Thus if
K\subset\mathsf{H}_{1}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
is a
\Gamma
-stable compact open subgroup, then there is a morphism of Shimura varieties
\mathbf{Sh}_{K^{\Gamma}}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}\to\mathbf{Sh}_{K}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}
, which induces a map (where the superscript
\Gamma
denotes taking
\Gamma
-fixed points)

\displaystyle\mathbf{Sh}_{K^{\Gamma}}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}\to\mathbf{Sh}_{K}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}^{\Gamma}.

(1.0.1)

This morphism can be arranged to be an isomorphism under minor hypotheses, see Theorem
3.6.4
. In this paper, we will prove extensions of this result to
p
-adic integral models. We hope that this link between the geometry of the integral model associated to
\mathsf{G}
and that associated to
\mathsf{H}_{1}
will prove to be a useful general tool in the arithmetic geometry of Shimura varieties. As a first step, our main results will be used in joint work in progress of the authors
[
33
]
to construct new exotic Hecke correspondences between the special fibers of different Shimura varieties.

1.1.
Main results

Let
{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}
be a Shimura datum of Hodge type with reflex field
\mathsf{E}
. Fix a prime
p
and a prime
v
of
\mathsf{E}
above
p
, let
E
be the completion of
\mathsf{E}
at
v
and let
\mathcal{O}_{E}
be its ring of integers. Let
G=\mathsf{G}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and let
\mathcal{G}
be a parahoric model of
G
over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

Let
\mathsf{F}
and
\Gamma
be as above, let
F=\mathsf{F}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and let
\mathcal{O}_{F}
be
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{F}}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. Let
\mathcal{H}_{1}
be the corresponding (quasi-)parahoric model of
H_{1}
(see Section
6.3
for the precise construction). Let
K_{p}=\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
and
K_{1,p}=\mathcal{H}_{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
. We will write
\mathscr{S}_{K_{1,p}}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}
for the integral model over
\mathcal{O}_{E}
of
\mathbf{Sh}_{K_{1,p}}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}
constructed by
[
4
, Theorem 4.2.3]
, cf.
[
24
, Theorem 4.5.2]
.

Let
\mathscr{S}_{K_{1,p}}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}^{\lozenge/}
be the v-sheaf associated to
\mathscr{S}_{K_{1,p}}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}
, see Section
2.3.3
. This is characterized by the existence of a morphism (functorial in the tuple
(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y},\mathcal{H}_{1})
) of v-stacks

\displaystyle\mathscr{S}_{K_{1,p}}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}^{\lozenge/}\to\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{H}_{1},\mu}.

(1.1.1)

In particular, this morphism is
\Gamma
-equivariant for the natural
\Gamma
-action on the source and target. We similarly have
\mathscr{S}_{K_{p}}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}^{\lozenge/}\to\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G},\mu}
and the obvious square is
2
-commutative.

Theorem 1

(Theorem
6.3.1
)
.

If
\Sha^{1}(\mathbb{Q},\mathsf{G})\to\Sha^{1}(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{G})
is injective,
then the following diagram of v-stacks is
2
-Cartesian

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{S}_{K_{p}^{\Gamma}}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}^{\lozenge/}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{S}_{K_{p,1}}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}^{\Gamma,\lozenge/}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G},\mu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{H}_{1},\mu}^{h\Gamma}.}

(1.1.2)

Here the superscript
h\Gamma
denotes the stacky (or homotopy) fixed points of a stack and the superscript
\Gamma
denotes the usual fixed points of a sheaf.

The assumption that
\Sha^{1}(\mathbb{Q},\mathsf{G})\to\Sha^{1}(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{G})
is injective is necessary, see Section
3.1
for an exploration of the theorem in the case of tori.

1.1.1.

The statement of Theorem
1
is essentially optimal, but it does not directly address when the map of schemes
\mathscr{S}_{K_{p}}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}\to\mathscr{S}_{K_{p,1}}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}^{\Gamma}
is an isomorphism. We address this more concrete question in the following theorem, phrased for finite level Shimura varieties. For a
\Gamma
-stable and neat compact open subgroup
K^{p}\subset\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
we write
K_{1}^{p}=K_{1}^{p}\cap H_{1}(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
and
K_{1}=K_{1}^{p}K_{1,p}
.

Theorem 2

(Theorem
5.1.2
)
.

Assume that
\mathsf{F}
is tamely ramified over
\mathbb{Q}
, that
\Sha^{1}(\mathbb{Q},\mathsf{G})\to\Sha^{1}(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{G})
is injective, that
p>2
and that
p
is unramified in
\mathsf{F}
. The following results hold.

(1)

There is a cofinal collection of
\Gamma
-stable compact open subgroups
K^{p}\subset\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
such that the natural map

\displaystyle\mathscr{S}_{K^{\Gamma}}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}\to\mathscr{S}_{K_{1}}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}^{\Gamma}

(1.1.3)

is a universal homeomorphism.

(2)

If
p
is coprime to
|\Gamma|\cdot|\pi_{1}(G^{\mathrm{der}})|
, and if
G
splits over a tamely ramified extension, then there is a cofinal collection of
K^{p}
as above such that the natural map (
1.1.3
) is an isomorphism.

(3)

If
K_{p}
is hyperspecial, then there is a cofinal collection of
K^{p}
as above such that the natural map (
1.1.3
) is an isomorphism.

The assumption that
G
splits over a tamely ramified extension in part (2) can be weakened, see Hypothesis
4.3.2
.

Example 1.1.2
.

If
{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}=(\operatorname{GL}_{2},\mathsf{H}^{\pm})
, then
{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}
is the subgroup of
\operatorname{Res}_{\mathsf{F}/\mathbb{Q}}\operatorname{GL}_{2}
consisting of those matrices with determinant in
\mathbb{G}_{m}\subset\operatorname{Res}_{\mathsf{F}/\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{G}_{m}
. The
p
-adic integral models for the Shimura varieties for
{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}
have a moduli interpretation in terms of (weakly polarized) abelian varieties
A
of dimension
[\mathsf{F}:\mathbb{Q}]
up to prime-to-
p
isogeny, equipped with an action
i:\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{F},(p)}\to\operatorname{End}(A)
. The action of an element
\gamma\in\Gamma
is then by precomposing
i
with
\gamma:\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{F},(p)}\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{F},(p)}
. The morphism from the modular curve can be thought of as taking an elliptic curve up to prime-to-
p
isogeny
E
, and forming the abelian variety up to isogeny
E\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{F},(p)}
together with its tautological
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{F},(p)}
action. The statement of Theorem
2
, up to keeping track of level structures, is then essentially an instance of étale Galois descent of modules for the étale cover
\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{F},(p)}\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
.
1
1

1

Indeed, one applies this to
A
considered as a sheaf on the category of schemes over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, with target the category of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{F},(p)}
-modules. Descent then tells us that a
\Gamma
-descent datum on
A
is induced from unique sheaf
E
of
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
-modules on the category of schemes over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, by
A=E\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{F},(p)}
.
If
p
is
not
unramified in
\mathsf{F}
, then
\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{F},(p)}\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
is not a Galois cover with Galois group
\Gamma
, and so this argument does not work.

1.1.3.
Igusa stacks

Igusa stacks are certain
p
-adic analytic objects (Artin v-stacks) associated to a Shimura datum
{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}
; they were conjectured to exist by Scholze. They were recently constructed by Zhang
[
38
]
in the PEL type case, and in
[
3
]
in the Hodge type case.

Let
{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}
be a Shimura datum of Hodge type and a place
v
above
p
of the reflex field
\mathsf{E}
of
{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}
. Let
\mathsf{F},\Gamma
and
{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}
be as above. Then there is a v-sheaf
\operatorname{Igs}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}
equipped with a
\Gamma
-equivariant map
\operatorname{Igs}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}\to\operatorname{Bun}_{H_{1}}
to the stack
\operatorname{Bun}_{H_{1}}
of
H_{1}
-bundles on the Fargues–Fontaine curve, see
[
3
, Theorem I]
. This map moreover factors through the open substack
\operatorname{Bun}_{H_{1},\mu^{-1}}\subset\operatorname{Bun}_{H_{1}}
corresponding to the set
B(H_{1},\mu^{-1})\subset B(H_{1})
of
\mu^{-1}
-admissible
\sigma
-conjugacy classes, where
\mu
is the
H_{1}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
conjugacy class of cocharacters of
H_{1}
induced by the Hodge cocharacter and the place
v
. We have similar objects for
{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}
.

Theorem 3

(Theorem
6.2.1
)
.

If
\Sha^{1}(\mathbb{Q},\mathsf{G})\to\Sha^{1}(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{G})
is injective, then the natural map

\displaystyle\operatorname{Igs}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}\to\operatorname{Igs}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}^{\Gamma}\times_{\left(\operatorname{Bun}_{H_{1},\mu^{-1}}\right)^{h\Gamma}}\operatorname{Bun}_{G,\mu^{-1}}

(1.1.4)

is an isomorphism.

Although we believe Theorem
3
to be of significant independent interest, it is also the crucial input in proving Theorem
1
. Indeed, the proof of Theorem
1
roughly proceeds first by proving that the fixed points of the v-sheaf associated to the rigid generic fiber of the Shimura variety are as expected, then using this input to prove Theorem
3
, then finally using this to deduce Theorem
1
. In this procedure the Igusa stack is the key linchpin which allows us to pass from information about fixed points on the generic fiber to information about the fixed points of the integral model. See Section
1.3
for more details.

1.2.
Motivation

Our motivation for proving Theorems
2
and
1
is that they can be used to reduce questions about the integral models for
{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}
to
\Gamma
-equivariant questions about the integral models for
{(\mathsf{H},\mathsf{Y})}
or
{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}
. This is useful, because given
{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}
and a prime
p
, one can always choose a Galois totally real field
\mathsf{F}
that is unramified at
p
such that
H\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and
H_{1}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}
are quasi-split.

For example, the Langlands–Rapoport conjecture for
{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}
is out of reach when
G\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}
is not quasi-split, because of the non-existence of CM lifts of isogeny classes. We expect that it is possible to establish a weak version of the Langlands–Rapoport
\tau
conjecture of
[
15
]
at non quasi-split primes, by reducing to the quasi-split case via Theorems
2
.

A second example: In forthcoming work, Xiao and Zhu
[
36
,
35
]
will construct exotic Hecke correspondences between the mod
p
fibers of different Shimura varieties of Hodge type at unramified and quasi-split primes. In their work, the Shimura varieties correspond to certain Shimura data
{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}
and
(\mathsf{G}^{\prime},\mathsf{X}^{\prime})
such that
\mathsf{G}
and
\mathsf{G}^{\prime}
are pure inner forms and such that
\mathsf{G}\otimes\mathbb{A}_{f}\simeq\mathsf{G}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{A}_{f}
. Their proof relies heavily on the fact that both
\mathsf{G}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and
\mathsf{G}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}
are quasi-split, through the existence of CM lifts of isogeny classes.

These correspondences conjecturally exist for certain pairs
{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}
and
(\mathsf{G}^{\prime},\mathsf{X}^{\prime})
, where
\mathsf{G}
and
\mathsf{G}^{\prime}
are pure inner forms, under the more general condition that
\mathsf{G}\otimes\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p}\simeq\mathsf{G}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p}
. In work in preparation of the authors, see
[
33
]
, we construct these more general exotic Hecke correspondences for primes
p
where
G
splits over an unramified extension (but is not necessarily quasi-split), by reducing to the quasi-split case via Theorem
2
.

1.3.
Proofs of the main theorems

The first step in the proofs of Theorems
1
,
2
and
3
is to compute the
\Gamma
-fixed points of Shimura varieties over
\mathbb{C}
.

To compute these fixed points, we argue on the level of
\mathbb{C}
-points and reduce to a concrete question about fixed points of adelic double quotients. To tackle the resulting questions, we use the methods of non-abelian group cohomology. These general methods tell us that the natural morphism of action groupoids
2
2

2

The group
{(\mathsf{H},\mathsf{Y})}
does typically not satisfy Milne’s axiom SV5, see Lemma
3.2.2
, and so the action of
\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{Q})
on
\mathsf{Y}\times\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{A}_{f})/K
is typically not free even for sufficiently small
K
.

\displaystyle\left[\mathsf{G}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{f})/K^{\Gamma}\right]\to\left[\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash\mathsf{Y}\times\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{A}_{f})/K\right]^{h\Gamma}

(1.3.1)

is an equivalence if
\Sha^{1}(\mathbb{Q},\mathsf{G})\to\Sha^{1}(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{G})
is injective and
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,K)=\{1\}
. If
\mathsf{F}
is tamely ramified over
\mathbb{Q}
, then we prove that there is a cofinal collection of
\Gamma
-stable
K\subset\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
with
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,K)
trivial, see Section
3.4
. Using some tricks, we then use this to compute the fixed points of
\mathbf{Sh}_{K_{1}}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}
, where
K_{1}=K\cap\mathsf{H}_{1}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
, see Section
3.6
.

1.3.1.

We now explain how to prove Theorem
2
. Since we know what happens on the generic fiber, we observe that it suffices to prove that
\mathscr{S}_{K_{1}}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}^{\Gamma}
is normal and flat over
\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{E}
. If the order of
\Gamma
is prime-to-
p
, then we will prove this by taking
\Gamma
-fixed points of the local model diagram for
\mathscr{S}_{K_{1}}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}
constructed by
[
13
]
, and using the fact that fixed points of smooth morphisms are again smooth. The problem then reduces to showing that
\Gamma
-fixed points of the local models for
{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}
give the local models for
{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}
. We deduce this from unramified base change for local models. Here we crucially use the assumption that
p
is unramified in
\mathsf{F}
. It would be interesting to understand the
\Gamma
-fixed points of the local models for
{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}
when
\mathsf{F}
is tamely ramified at
p
.

When
p
divides the order of
\Gamma
, we can only show that the weak normalization of
\mathscr{S}_{K_{1}}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}^{\Gamma}
is normal and flat over
\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{E}
. For this, we may argue on the level of the corresponding v-sheaves, where we reduce it to proving that the
\Gamma
-fixed points of the integral local Shimura varieties for
H
give the integral local Shimura varieties for
G
. Here we crucially use the assumption that
p
is unramified in
\mathsf{F}
, as in this setting it is straightforward to calculate the fixed points of the relevant integral local Shimura varieties via the fixed points of stacks of local shtukas. On the other hand, using Theorem
1
, if this local problem can be solved in greater generality one could obtain a concomitant strengthening of Theorem
2
.

1.3.2.

We now explain how to prove Theorem
3
; let us assume for simplicity that the Shimura varieties in question are proper. The Igusa stack for
{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}
sits by
[
3
, Thm I]
in a
2
-Cartesian diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Sh}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}_{E}^{\lozenge}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Gr}_{H_{1},\mu^{-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Igs}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Bun}_{H_{1},\mu^{-1}}.}

(1.3.2)

We now take
\Gamma
-homotopy-fixed points of the four objects in the square (the resulting square is again
2
-Cartesian by Lemma
A.1.11
). The top row of the fixed point diagram is given by
\mathbf{Sh}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}_{E}^{\lozenge}\to\operatorname{Gr}_{G,\mu^{-1}}
; this uses our fixed point result for the generic fiber of the Shimura variety and is straightforward for the target of the Hodge–Tate period map. If we base change via
\operatorname{Bun}_{G,\mu^{-1}}\to\operatorname{Bun}_{H_{1},\mu^{-1}}
, we get the
2
-Cartesian diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Sh}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}_{E}^{\lozenge}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Gr}_{G,\mu^{-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\operatorname{Igs}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})})^{\Gamma}\times_{\operatorname{Bun}_{H_{1},\mu^{-1}}^{h\Gamma}}\operatorname{Bun}_{G,\mu^{-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Bun}_{G,\mu^{-1}}.}

(1.3.3)

The Igusa stack for
{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}
sits in the same Cartesian diagram via the natural map
\operatorname{Igs}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}\to\operatorname{Igs}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}
. To see that this natural map is an isomorphism, we use the fact that
\operatorname{Gr}_{G,\mu^{-1}}\to\operatorname{Bun}_{G,\mu^{-1}}
is a covering map to reduce it to checking that
\mathbf{Sh}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}_{E}^{\lozenge}\to\mathbf{Sh}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}_{E}^{\Gamma,\lozenge}
is an isomorphism. This latter map is an isomorphism by the argument of Section
1.3.1
.

1.3.3.

In the proper case, Theorem
1
follows from Theorem
3
and the compatible
2
-Cartesian diagrams of
[
3
, Thm VII, Remark 6.0.2]

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{S}_{K_{p}}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}^{\lozenge/}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G},\mu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Igs}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Bun}_{G,\mu^{-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{S}_{K_{p,1}}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}^{\lozenge/}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{H}_{1},\mu,\delta=1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Igs}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Bun}_{H_{1},\mu^{-1}}.}

(1.3.4)

The subscript
\delta=1
in
\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{H}_{1},\mu,\delta=1}
deals with the technical issue that
\mathcal{H}_{1}
could genuinely be a quasi-parahoric and not a parahoric. Thus the only property of integral models going into our proof of Theorem
2
is the fiber product diagram of (
1.3.4
).

1.3.4.

In order to facilitate the arguments outlined above, it is important for us to verify that many constructions in the theory of integral models of Shimura varieties (shtukas, local models, etc.) are functorial in the triple
{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X},\mathcal{G})}
in a
2
-categorical sense. For example, we show that the morphisms
\mathscr{S}_{K}{(\mathsf{H},\mathsf{Y})}^{\lozenge/}\to\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{H},\mu}
from the (modified) diamond associated to an integral model of a Shimura variety, see Section
2.3.3
, to the stack of
\mathcal{G}
-shtukas of type
\mu
of Pappas–Rapoport
[
24
]
, can be upgraded to a weak natural transformation of weak functors (or pseudo-functors), see Proposition
4.1.4
and Corollary
4.2.2
. This is necessary because this implies that there is an induced map

\displaystyle\mathscr{S}_{K}{(\mathsf{H},\mathsf{Y})}^{\lozenge/,\Gamma}\to\left(\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{H},\mu}\right)^{h\Gamma},

(1.3.5)

which features in the statement of Theorem
1
. In the proofs of Theorems
1
and
3
, we make use of the
2
-categorical theory of non-abelian Galois cohomology theory for the
(2,1)
-category of stacks on a site. We develop this theory from scratch in Appendix
A
.

1.4.
Outline of the paper

In Section
2
we discuss
preliminaries on perfectoid geometry. We also recall local models and local shtukas and compute the fixed points of local models and integral local Shimura varieties. In Section
3
we prove Theorem
1
on the generic fiber. In Section
4
we discuss integral models of Shimura varieties following Pappas–Rapoport
[
24
]
, and construct a
2
-categorical enhancement of their shtukas. In Section
4.3
, we discuss local model diagrams for Shimura varieties of Hodge type following
[
16
]
, and prove
\Gamma
-equivariance. In Section
5
we prove Theorem
2
. In Section
6
we introduce Igusa stacks, prove Theorem
3
and deduce Theorem
1
.

In Appendix
A
we develop some 2-category theory to be used throughout the paper. The main question we answers is when taking (2-categorical) fixed points commutes with taking (2-categorical) quotients.

1.5.
Acknowledgements

This project started while both authors were attending the 2022 IHES summer school on the Langlands Program, and we would like to thank the IHES for providing excellent working conditions. We would also like to thank Ana Caraiani, Brian Conrad, Sean Cotner, Toby Gee, Richard Taylor, Lie Qian, Rong Zhou and Xinwen Zhu for helpful discussions. In addition, large portions of this work were written up while the first author was a visitor at Imperial College London, and while the second author was a visitor at Stanford University. The authors would like to thank both of these institutions for their hospitality.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
2
-category theory

At many points in this article, we will be taking fixed points for the action of a finite group
\Gamma
on a stack
\mathcal{X}
. The most reasonable way of doing this seems to be taking the
2
-categorical or homotopy fixed points. For example, if
\mathsf{F}
is a finite Galois extension of
\mathbb{Q}
with Galois group
\Gamma
, then
\Gamma
acts on the category of
\mathsf{F}
-vector spaces by twisting the
\mathsf{F}
-action, and the
\Gamma
-homotopy fixed points can be identified with the category of
\mathbb{Q}
-vector spaces; this is a restatement of Galois descent along
\operatorname{Spec}\mathsf{F}\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Q}
.

We will refer the reader to Appendix
A
for the definition of a stack (or category) with
\Gamma
-action and the notion of a
\Gamma
-equivariant morphism of stacks (or categories) with
\Gamma
-action, see Definition
A.1.3
. In particular, we note that it is
not
a property of a morphism to be
\Gamma
-equivariant, but rather an extra structure. To emphasize this, we will sometimes call such morphisms
\Gamma
-equivariant in the
2
-categorical sense
.

2.2.
Non-abelian cohomology

Given a group
\Gamma
acting on a (possibly non-abelian) group
H
, we denote by
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,H)
the pointed set given by
1
-cocycles
\sigma:\Gamma\to H
up to
H
-conjugacy. We begin with the following simple non-abelian Shapiro’s lemma, which appears in
[
31
, Section 5.8.(b)]
as an exercise left to the reader.

Lemma 2.2.1
.

Let
\Gamma
be a group and let
H
be a group with an action of a subgroup
\Gamma^{\prime}
of
\Gamma
. Then if
X:=\text{Map}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(\Gamma,H)
with the natural left action
\gamma_{0}((h_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in\Gamma})=(h_{\gamma\cdot\gamma_{0}})_{\gamma\in\Gamma}
we have a natural isomorphism
\psi:\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,X)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma^{\prime},H)
.

2.3.
Background on perfectoid geometry

In this section we recall some background on perfectoid spaces. We refer the reader to
[
24
]
and
[
29
]
for details.

Let
k
be a perfect field of characteristic
p
and write
\operatorname{Perf}_{k}
for the category of perfectoid spaces over
k
. If
k=\mathbb{F}_{p}
we write
\operatorname{Perf}=\operatorname{Perf}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
. For any perfectoid space
S
over
k
, we write
S\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\times}\limits^{\boldsymbol{.}}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
for the analytic adic space defined in
[
29
, Proposition 11.2.1]
. In particular, when
S=\operatorname{Spa}(R,R^{+})
is affinoid perfectoid,
S\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\times}\limits^{\boldsymbol{.}}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is given by

\displaystyle S\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\times}\limits^{\boldsymbol{.}}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}=\operatorname{Spa}(W(R^{+}))\setminus\{[\varpi]=0\},

(2.3.1)

where
W(R^{+})
denotes the ring of
p
-typical Witt vectors of the perfect ring
R^{+}
, and where
[\varpi]
denotes the Teichmülller lift to
W(R^{+})
of a pseudo-uniformiser
\varpi
in
R^{+}
. The Frobenius for
W(R^{+})
restricts to give a Frobenius operator
\mathrm{Frob}_{S}
on
S\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\times}\limits^{\boldsymbol{.}}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. By
[
29
, Proposition 11.3.1]
, any untilt
S^{\sharp}
of
S
determines a closed Cartier divisor
S^{\sharp}\hookrightarrow S\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\times}\limits^{\boldsymbol{.}}}\operatorname{Spa}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. For
S
in
\operatorname{Perf}
we define also
\mathcal{Y}(S)=S\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\times}\limits^{\boldsymbol{.}}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\setminus\{p=0\}
.

For any
S
in
\operatorname{Perf}
, the relative adic Fargues–Fontaine curve over
S
is the quotient

\displaystyle X_{S}=\mathcal{Y}(S)/\varphi^{\mathbb{Z}}.

(2.3.2)

By
[
8
, Proposition II.1.16]
, the action of
\mathrm{Frob}_{S}
on
\mathcal{Y}(R,R^{+})
is free and totally discontinuous, which means that the quotient is well-defined. Let
G
be a reductive group over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. Following
[
8
]
, we denote by
\operatorname{Bun}_{G}(S)
the groupoid of
G
-torsors on
X_{S}
. By
[
8
, Proposition III.1.3]
,
\operatorname{Bun}_{G}
is a small v-stack on
\operatorname{Perf}
. A morphism
f:G\to G^{\prime}
induces a
1
-morphism
\operatorname{Bun}_{G}\to\operatorname{Bun}_{G^{\prime}}
by pushing out torsors, and the following lemma follows from Lemma
A.2.1
.

Lemma 2.3.1
.

The construction of the v-stack
\operatorname{Bun}_{G}
for
G
a reductive group scheme
over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
extends to a weak functor

\{\text{reductive groups over }\mathbb{Q}_{p}\}\to\{v\text{-stacks
on }\operatorname{Perf}\};\quad G\mapsto\operatorname{Bun}_{G}.

2.3.2.

Let
B(G)
be the set of
\sigma
-conjugacy classes in
G(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
, equipped with the topology coming from the
opposite
of the partial order defined in
[
27
, Section 2.3]
. By
[
34
, Theorem 1]
, there is a homeomorphism

\displaystyle|\operatorname{Bun}_{G}|\to B(G).

(2.3.3)

If
\mu
is a
G(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
-conjugacy class of minuscule cocharacters, we let
B(G,\mu^{-1})\subset B(G)
be the set of
\mu^{-1}
-admissible elements, as defined in
[
14
, Section 1.1.5]
; note that this set is closed in the partial order and thus defines an open substack

\operatorname{Bun}_{G,\mu^{-1}}\subset\operatorname{Bun}_{G},

via
[
30
, Proposition 12.9]
.

2.3.3.

If
X
is an adic space over
\operatorname{Spa}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
, let
X^{\lozenge}
denote the set-valued functor on
\operatorname{Perf}
given by

\displaystyle X^{\lozenge}(S)=\{(S^{\sharp},f)\}/\sim

(2.3.4)

for any
S
in
\operatorname{Perf}
, where
S^{\sharp}
is an untilt of
S
and
f:S^{\sharp}\to X
is a morphism of adic spaces. This determines a v-sheaf on
\operatorname{Perf}
by
[
29
, Lemma 18.1.1]
. For a Huber pair
(A,A^{+})
over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, we write
\operatorname{Spd}(A,A^{+})
in place of
\operatorname{Spa}(A,A^{+})^{\lozenge}
, and when
A^{+}=A^{\circ}
we write
\operatorname{Spd}(A)
instead of
\operatorname{Spd}(A,A^{+})
. In particular, we see that
\operatorname{Spd}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
parametrizes isomorphism classes of untilts, cf.
[
29
, Definition 10.1.3]
.

If
\mathcal{X}
is a formal scheme over
\operatorname{Spf}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, then we can consider it as an adic space over
\operatorname{Spa}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
using
[
28
, Proposition 2.2.1]
and use that adic space to define
\mathcal{X}^{\lozenge}
. This defines a functor from formal schemes over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
to v-sheaves over
\operatorname{Spd}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. This functor is fully faithful when restricted to absolutely weakly normal formal schemes that are flat, separated and formally of finite type over
\operatorname{Spf}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, see
[
1
, Theorem 2.16]
.

If
X
is a scheme over
\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, then there are three possible v-sheaves that can be associated to
X
: There is
X^{\lozenge}
, there is
X^{\lozenge/}
and there is
X^{\diamond}
, see
[
1
, Definition 2.10]
and
[
24
, Definition 2.1.9]
. By
[
24
, Corollary 2.1.8]
, the functor
X\mapsto X^{\lozenge/}
is fully faithful when restricted to schemes that are flat normal and separated locally of finite type over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

For schemes
X
that are locally of finite type over
\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, these admit the following descriptions, see
[
1
, Remark 2.11]
:

•

The v-sheaf
X^{\diamond}
can be identified with
\left(\widehat{X}\right)^{\lozenge}
, where
\widehat{X}
is the
p
-adic formal scheme over
\operatorname{Spf}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
given by the completion of
X
in its special fiber. In
[
24
]
, they write
X^{\blacklozenge}
for
X^{\diamond}
.

•

The v-sheaf
X^{\lozenge}
can be identified with
(X^{\mathrm{an}})^{\lozenge}
, where
X^{\mathrm{an}}
is the ‘analytification’ of
X
, see
[
1
, Remark 2.11]
.

•

The v-sheaf
X^{\lozenge/}
is created by gluing
X^{\diamond}
to
X^{\lozenge}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
along the open immersion
X^{\diamond}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\to X^{\lozenge}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
.

2.4.
Local models

In this section we let
G
be a connected reductive group over a finite extension
L
of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and let
\mu
be a
G(\overline{L})
-conjugacy class of minuscule cocharacters of
G
with reflex field
E\subset\overline{L}
. We fix a quasi-parahoric
3
3

3

In the sense of
[
25
, Section 2.2]
model
\mathcal{G}
of
G
over
\mathcal{O}_{L}
. We let
\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathcal{G}}\to\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{L}
be the Beilinson–Drinfeld affine flag variety of
\mathcal{G}
, see
[
1
, Section 4.1]
, considered as a v-sheaf on
\operatorname{Perf}
. There is a closed subfunctor

\displaystyle\operatorname{Gr}_{G,\mu}\subset\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathcal{G}}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{L}}\operatorname{Spd}E,

(2.4.1)

see
[
1
, Corollary 4.6]
, whose closure in
\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathcal{G}}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{L}}\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{E}
defines a closed subfunctor

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{G},\mu}^{\mathrm{v}}\subset\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathcal{G}}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{L}}\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{E},

(2.4.2)

called the v-sheaf local model attached to
(\mathcal{G},\mu)
.

2.4.1.

Assume from now on that
\mathcal{G}
is a parahoric and let
\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{ad}}
be the corresponding parahoric model for
\mathsf{G}^{\mathrm{ad}}
. The formation of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{G},\mu}^{\mathrm{v}}
is functorial for morphisms of pairs
(\mathcal{G}_{1},\mu_{1})\to(\mathcal{G}_{2},\mu_{2})
where
\mathcal{G}_{1},\mathcal{G}_{2}
are parahoric, and preserves closed embeddings by
[
1
, Proposition 4.16]
. Moreover, the morphism

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{G},\mu}^{\mathrm{v}}\to\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{ad}},\mu}^{\mathrm{v}}

(2.4.3)

induced by
(\mathcal{G},\mu)\to(\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{ad}},\mu)
is an isomorphism.

2.4.2.

Let
L^{\prime}\subset\overline{L}
be a finite extension of
L
and let
E^{\prime}\supset L^{\prime}
be the reflex field of
\mu
considered as a
G(\overline{L})
-conjugacy class of cocharacters of
G_{L^{\prime}}
.

Lemma 2.4.3
.

There is a natural isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{G},\mu}^{\mathrm{v}}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{E}}\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{E^{\prime}}\to\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{O}_{L^{\prime}}},\mu}^{\mathrm{v}}.

(2.4.4)

Proof.

The analogous result for

\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathcal{G}}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{L^{\prime}}}\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{L^{\prime}}\to\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{O}_{L^{\prime}}}}

follows directly from the definition, see
[
1
, Section 4.1]
. It also follows directly from the definition that this induces a natural isomorphism

\displaystyle\operatorname{Gr}_{G,\mu}\times_{\operatorname{Spa}E}\operatorname{Spa}E^{\prime}\to\operatorname{Gr}_{G_{L^{\prime}},\mu}.

(2.4.5)

Since the map
\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{E^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{O}_{E}
is finite flat, it is in particular proper and open. Thus the map
\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{E^{\prime}}\to\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{E}
is partially proper and open. It then follows from
[
9
, Corollary 2.9]
, that the formation of v-sheaf closures commutes with basechanging from
\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{E}
to
\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{E^{\prime}}
. Thus the natural map

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{G},\mu}^{\mathrm{v}}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{E}}\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{E^{\prime}}\to\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{O}_{L^{\prime}}},\mu}^{\mathrm{v}}.

(2.4.6)

is an isomorphism.
∎

Now suppose that
L^{\prime}/L
is Galois and let
\Gamma
be its Galois group. Define
\mathcal{H}:=\operatorname{Res}_{\mathcal{O}_{L^{\prime}}/\mathcal{O}_{L}}\mathcal{G}
and let
\mu
be the induced conjugacy class of cocharacters of
H:=\mathcal{H}_{L}
. The natural map
(\mathcal{G},\mu)\to(\mathcal{H},\mu)
induces a natural map

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{G},\mu}^{\mathrm{v}}\to\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{H},\mu}^{\mathrm{v}},

(2.4.7)

Lemma 2.4.4
.

If
L^{\prime}/L
is unramified, then there is a
\Gamma
-equivariant isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{H},\mu}^{\mathrm{v}}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{E}}\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{E^{\prime}}\to\prod_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{G},\mu}^{\mathrm{v}}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{E}}\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{E^{\prime}},

(2.4.8)

under which the natural map from equation
2.4.7
corresponds to the inclusion of the diagonal.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.4.3
, there is a natural (in particular
\Gamma
-equivariant) isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{H},\mu}^{\mathrm{v}}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{E}}\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{E^{\prime}}\to\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{O}_{L^{\prime}}},\mu}^{\mathrm{v}}.

(2.4.9)

There is a
\Gamma
-equivariant isomorphism
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{O}_{L^{\prime}}}\to\prod_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{O}_{L}^{\prime}}
since
L^{\prime}/L
is unramified. Since the formation of local models commutes with direct products, see
[
1
, Proposition 4.16]
, this induces a
\Gamma
-equivariant isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{O}_{L^{\prime}}},\mu}^{\mathrm{v}}\to\prod_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{O}_{L^{\prime}}},\mu}^{\mathrm{v}}.

(2.4.10)

Using Lemma
2.4.3
again, we again identify the right-hand side with

\displaystyle\prod_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{G},\mu}^{\mathrm{v}}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{E}}\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{E^{\prime}}.

(2.4.11)

Moreover, under these identifications the natural map

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{G},\mu}^{\mathrm{v}}\to\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{H},\mu}^{\mathrm{v}}

(2.4.12)

corresponds to the inclusion of the diagonal into the product.
∎

2.4.5.

Now let
\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{L^{\prime}}\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{L}
be a finite étale Galois cover with Galois group
\Gamma
and generic fiber
\operatorname{Spec}L^{\prime}\to\operatorname{Spec}L
. Define
\mathcal{H}:=\operatorname{Res}_{\mathcal{O}_{L^{\prime}}/\mathcal{O}_{L}}\mathcal{G}
and let
\mu
be the induced conjugacy class of cocharacters of
H:=\mathcal{H}_{L}
. Then
\Gamma
-acts on
\mathcal{H}
in a way that preserves
\mu
and thus acts on
\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{H},\mu}^{\mathrm{v}}
. The natural map of (
2.4.7
) is
\Gamma
-equivariant for the trivial
\Gamma
-action on the source.

Let
\mathfrak{p}
be a maximal ideal of
\mathcal{O}_{L^{\prime}}
, let
\mathcal{O}_{L^{\prime\prime}}
be the local ring of
\mathcal{O}_{L^{\prime}}
at
\mathfrak{p}
with fraction field
L^{\prime\prime}
. Let
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\subset\Gamma
be the stabilizer of
\mathfrak{p}
, which is also the Galois group of
L^{\prime\prime}/L
. Choose an embedding
L^{\prime\prime}\to\overline{L}
and let
E^{\prime\prime}
be the reflex field of
\mu
considered as an
G_{L^{\prime\prime}}(\overline{L})
-conjugacy class of cocharacters of
G_{L^{\prime\prime}}
.

Lemma 2.4.6
.

There is a
\Gamma
-equivariant isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{H},\mu}^{\mathrm{v}}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{E}}\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{E^{\prime\prime}}\to\prod_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{G},\mu}^{\mathrm{v}}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{E}}\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{E^{\prime}},

(2.4.13)

under which the natural map from equation
2.4.7
corresponds to the inclusion of the diagonal.

Proof.

Since
\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{L^{\prime}}\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{L}
is Galois there is a
\Gamma
-equivariant isomorphism

\displaystyle\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{L^{\prime}}\to\hom_{\Gamma^{\prime\prime}}(\Gamma,\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{L^{\prime\prime}}).

(2.4.14)

Similarly there is a
\Gamma
-equivariant isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}\to\hom_{\Gamma^{\prime\prime}}(\Gamma,\operatorname{Res}_{\mathcal{O}_{L^{\prime\prime}}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{O}_{L^{\prime\prime}}})\simeq\prod_{\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\backslash\Gamma}\operatorname{Res}_{\mathcal{O}_{L^{\prime\prime}}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{O}_{L^{\prime\prime}}}.

(2.4.15)

Since the formation of local models commutes with products by
[
9
, Corollary 2.9]
, the lemma reduces to Lemma
2.4.4
.
∎

2.4.7.

Recall that there are unique (up to unique isomorphism) flat and (absolutely) weakly normal schemes
\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{G},\mu}
over
\mathcal{O}_{E}
with associated v-sheaf isomorphic to
\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{G},\mu}^{\mathrm{v}}
, by
[
1
, Theorem 1.11]
.

Proposition 2.4.8
.

The natural map of equation (
2.4.7
) induces an isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{G},\mu}\to\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{H},\mu}^{\Gamma}.

(2.4.16)

Proof.

By
[
1
, Proposition 2.18]
, the functor sending a proper flat absolutely weakly normal scheme over
\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{L}
to its associated v-sheaf is fully faithful. This functor moreover commutes with base change along
\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{E^{\prime\prime}}\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{E}
and with fiber products. Thus by Lemma
2.4.6
there is a
\Gamma
-equivariant isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{H},\mu}\times_{\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{E}}\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{E^{\prime\prime}}\to\prod_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{G},\mu}\times_{\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{E}}\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{E^{\prime\prime}}

(2.4.17)

under which the natural map
\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{G},\mu}\to\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{H},\mu}
corresponds to the inclusion of the diagonal. The proposition follows immediately from this description.
∎

Corollary 2.4.9
.

The natural map

\displaystyle\left[\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{G},\mu}/\mathcal{G}\right]\to\left[\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{H},\mu}/\mathcal{H}\right]^{h\Gamma}

(2.4.18)

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

After basechanging to
\operatorname{Spa}(\mathcal{O}_{L})
we have an isomorphism
\mathcal{H}\simeq\hom(\Gamma,\mathcal{G})
and thus it follows from Lemma
2.2.1
that
\underline{H}^{1}(\Gamma,\mathcal{H})
vanishes. The corollary now follows from Proposition
2.4.8
and Corollary
A.2.13
.
∎

2.5.
Shtukas

In this section we let
G
be a connected reductive group over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and
\mu
a
G(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
-conjugacy class of minuscule cocharacters of
G
with reflex field
E\subset\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
. We fix a quasi-parahoric model
\mathcal{G}
of
G
over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

Recall from
[
24
, Definition 2.4.3]
that a
\mathcal{G}
-shtuka over a perfectoid space
S
with leg at an untilt
S^{\sharp}
is defined to be a quadruple:
(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{P},\phi_{\mathcal{P}},\kappa)
where
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{P}
are
\mathcal{G}
-torsors over the analytic adic space
S\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\times}\limits^{\boldsymbol{.}}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, where
\kappa:\mathcal{Q}\to\left(\operatorname{Frob}_{S}\times 1\right)^{\ast}\mathcal{P}
is an isomorphism of
\mathcal{G}
-torsors and where

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathcal{P}}:{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathcal{Q}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{S\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\times}\limits^{\boldsymbol{.}}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\setminus S^{\sharp}}}\to{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathcal{P}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{S\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\times}\limits^{\boldsymbol{.}}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\setminus S^{\sharp}}}

(2.5.1)

is an isomorphism of
\mathcal{G}
-torsors over
S\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\times}\limits^{\boldsymbol{.}}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\setminus S^{\sharp}
. Here
S^{\sharp}\subset S\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\times}\limits^{\boldsymbol{.}}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is the Cartier divisor coming from the untilt
S^{\sharp}
. To be precise, here we consider
\left(\operatorname{Frob}_{S}\times 1\right)^{\ast}\mathcal{P}
as a
\mathcal{G}
-torsor via the isomorphism
\left(\operatorname{Frob}_{S}\times 1\right)^{\ast}\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}
coming from the fact that
\mathcal{G}
is defined over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. Note that the data of
\mathcal{Q}
and
\kappa
is superfluous in our definition, but it will be useful for us later to keep track of this information. In Section
4
we will often omit
\mathcal{Q}
and
\kappa
from the notation

2.5.1.

Let
\operatorname{Perf}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
be the category of perfectoid spaces
S
of characteristic
p
equipped with a map
S^{\lozenge}\to\operatorname{Spd}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, equipped with its v-topology. Let
\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G}}
be the stack of
\mathcal{G}
-shtukas over
\operatorname{Perf}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
, considered as a category fibered in groupoids over
\operatorname{Perf}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
. Explicitly, this means that it is the category whose objects are quintuples
(S\to\operatorname{Spd}\mathbb{Z}_{p},\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{P},\phi_{\mathcal{P}},\kappa)
, where
S\to\operatorname{Spd}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is an object of
\operatorname{Perf}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
and
(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{P},\phi_{\mathcal{P}},\kappa)
is a
\mathcal{G}
-shtuka over
S
. A morphism
f:(S\to\operatorname{Spd}\mathbb{Z}_{p},\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{P},\phi_{\mathcal{P}},\kappa)\to(S^{\prime}\to\operatorname{Spd}\mathbb{Z}_{p},\mathcal{Q}^{\prime},\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\phi_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}},\kappa^{\prime})
is a triple
(f,f_{\mathcal{P}},f_{\mathcal{Q}})
consisting of a morphism
f:S\to S^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant morphisms
f_{\mathcal{P}}:\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{P}^{\prime},f_{\mathcal{Q}}:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
fitting in a pair of Cartesian diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\times}\limits^{\boldsymbol{.}}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}\dot{\times}\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathcal{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times 1}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\times}\limits^{\boldsymbol{.}}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}\dot{\times}\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathcal{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times 1}

(2.5.2)

such that the following diagrams commute

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathcal{Q}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{S\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\times}\limits^{\boldsymbol{.}}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\setminus S^{\sharp}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathcal{P}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{S\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\times}\limits^{\boldsymbol{.}}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\setminus S^{\sharp}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathcal{P}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{S^{\prime}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}\setminus S^{\sharp}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathcal{Q^{\prime}}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{S^{\prime}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}\setminus S^{\sharp}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\mathcal{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathcal{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathcal{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\operatorname{Frob}_{S}\times 1\right)^{\ast}\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\operatorname{Frob}_{S^{\prime}}\times 1\right)^{\ast}\mathcal{P}^{\prime},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathcal{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{\prime}}

(2.5.3)

where the right vertical arrow in the second diagram is the arrow induced by
f_{\mathcal{P}}
. Since
\mu
is defined over
E
, there is a closed substack
4
4

4

Here by
\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes_{\operatorname{Spd}\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{E}
we mean the category fibered in groupoids over
\operatorname{Perf}_{\mathcal{O}_{E}}
given by the fiber product construction of
[
32
, Lemma 0040]
.

\displaystyle\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G}}\subset\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes_{\operatorname{Spd}\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{E},

(2.5.4)

defined as those shtukas where
\phi_{\mathcal{P}}
has relative position bounded by the v-sheaf local model

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{G},\mu}^{\mathrm{v}}\subset\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes_{\operatorname{Spd}\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{E},

(2.5.5)

see
[
24
, Definition 2.4.4]
. It is clear that a homomorphism
f:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
of quasi-parahoric group schemes induces a morphism.

\displaystyle f:\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G}}\to\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}

(2.5.6)

by pushing out torsors. Noting that the reflex field
E^{\prime}
of
\mu^{\prime}
is contained in
E
, this restricts to a morphism

\displaystyle\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G}}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{E}}\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{E^{\prime}}\to\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\mu^{\prime}},

(2.5.7)

if
f(\mu)=\mu^{\prime}
. This upgrades to a
2
-categorical statement as follows: Fix a field
L\subset\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
and let
\operatorname{ShtPr}_{L}
be the category of pairs
(\mathcal{G},\mu)
such that the reflex field
E(\mu)
of
\mu
is contained in
L
, and where the morphisms are the obvious morphisms of pairs. We will often write
E
for
E(\mu)
if
\mu
is clear from the context. We let
\mathcal{D}^{\circ}_{L}
be the strict
(2,1)
-category of categories fibered in groupoids over
\operatorname{Perf}_{\mathcal{O}_{L}}
and
\mathcal{D}_{L}
the full subcategory of categories fibered in groupoids over
\operatorname{Perf}_{L}
.

Lemma 2.5.2
.

There is a weak functor
\operatorname{Sht}:\operatorname{ShtPr}_{L}\to\mathcal{D}^{\circ}_{L}
, which on objects sends
(\mathcal{G},\mu)
to
\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G},\mu}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{E}}\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{L}
and which sends a morphism
(\mathcal{G},\mu)\to(\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
to the induced morphism
\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G},\mu}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{E}}\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{L}\to\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G},\mu}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{E^{\prime}}}\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{L}
induced by pushing out torsors along
\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

A morphism
f:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
induces a morphism
\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G}}\to\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}
by sending
(S\to\operatorname{Spd}\mathbb{Z}_{p},\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{P},\phi_{\mathcal{P}},\kappa)
to
(S\to\operatorname{Spd}\mathbb{Z}_{p},\mathcal{Q}\times^{\mathcal{G}}\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\mathcal{P}\times^{\mathcal{G}}\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\phi_{\mathcal{P}\times^{\mathcal{G}}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}},\kappa^{\prime}
). Here
\kappa^{\prime}
is the unique isomorphism induced by
\kappa
, using the fact that
f:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is defined over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and thus commutes with
\mathrm{Frob}_{S}
. To turn this into a weak functor, we use the coherence data for pushing out torsors coming from the proof of Lemma
A.2.1
.

As discussed above, this morphism restricts to a morphism between
\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G},\mu}
and
\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\mu^{\prime}}
and thus induces a morphism
\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G},\mu}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{E}}\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{L}\to\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\mu^{\prime}}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{E^{\prime}}}\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{L}
.
∎

We recall the open and closed substack
\mathrm{Sht}_{\mathcal{G},\mu,\delta=1}\subset\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G},\mu}
introduced in
[
4
, Section 3.3.8]
. If
\mathcal{G}
has connected special fiber (so
\mathcal{G}
is parahoric instead of just quasi-parahoric), then
\mathrm{Sht}_{\mathcal{G},\mu,\delta=1}=\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G},\mu}
.

2.6.
Integral local Shimura varieties

In this section we let
G
be a connected reductive group over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and
\mu
a
G(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
-conjugacy class of minuscule cocharacters of
G
with reflex field
E\subset\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
. We fix a quasi-parahoric model
\mathcal{G}
of
G
over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. It follows from Lemma
A.2.1
that there is a weak functor
\operatorname{Bun}
from the category of reductive groups over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
to the strict
(2,1)
-category of v-stacks on
\operatorname{Perf}
. Recall that there is a faithful morphism
\operatorname{BL}^{\circ}:\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G},\mu}\to\operatorname{Bun}_{G}
, as explained in
[
24
, Section 2.2.2]
, see
[
3
, Section 2.3.3]
.

Lemma 2.6.1
.

There is a weak natural transformation
\operatorname{Sht}\to\operatorname{Bun}
of weak functors
\operatorname{ShtPr}_{L}\to\operatorname{Stk}_{\operatorname{Perf}}
, giving
\operatorname{BL}^{\circ}:\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G},\mu}\to\operatorname{Bun}_{G}
on objects.

Proof.

This comes down to checking that pushing out torsors is compatible with descending them from
\mathcal{Y}_{[r,\infty)}(S)
(notation as in
[
24
, Section 2.2.2]
) to
X_{S}
, which is straightforward.
∎

For
b:\operatorname{Spd}\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}\to\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G},\mu}
we define
\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathcal{G},b,\mu}
as the
2
-fiber product

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathcal{G},b,\mu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G},\mu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spd}\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Bun}_{G}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

(2.6.1)

The universal property of the fiber product induces a natural map
x_{0}:\operatorname{Spd}\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}\to\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathcal{G},b,\mu}
. Note that a morphism of triples
(\mathcal{G},\mu,b)\to(\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\mu^{\prime},b^{\prime})
induces a morphism
\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathcal{G},b,\mu}\to\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime},b^{\prime},\mu^{\prime}}
taking
x_{0}
to
x_{0}^{\prime}
. It is straightforward to check that this gives a functor from the category of triples
(\mathcal{G},\mu,b)
with reflex field contained in
L
, to
\mathcal{D}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{O}_{L}}
, sending

\displaystyle(\mathcal{G},\mu,b)\mapsto\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathcal{G},b,\mu}\otimes_{\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{E}}\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{L}.

(2.6.2)

2.6.2.
Fixed points of integral local Shimura varieties

Let
\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{L}\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
be a finite étale Galois cover with Galois group
\Gamma
and generic fiber
\operatorname{Spec}L\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. Let
\mathcal{G}
be a quasi-parahoric model of
G
as before and define
\mathcal{H}:=\operatorname{Res}_{\mathcal{O}_{L}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{O}_{L}}
and let
\mu
be the induced conjugacy class of cocharacters of
H:=\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
. Then
\Gamma
-acts on
\mathcal{H}
in a way that preserves
\mu
and thus acts on
\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{H},\mu}\to\operatorname{Perf}_{\mathcal{O}_{E}}
by Lemma
2.5.2
.

Lemma 2.6.3
.

Let
X
be an adic space over
\operatorname{Spa}(\mathbb{Z}_{p},\mathbb{Z}_{p})
, then cocycles
\sigma:\Gamma\to\mathcal{H}(X)
are trivial étale locally on
X
.

Proof.

After basechanging to
\operatorname{Spa}(\mathcal{O}_{L},\mathcal{O}_{L})
, we have an isomorphism
\mathcal{H}\simeq\hom(\Gamma,\mathcal{G})
and the result then follows from Lemma
2.2.1
.
∎

Corollary 2.6.4
.

Consider the stack
\mathbb{B}\mathcal{H}
on the category of adic spaces over
\operatorname{Spa}(\mathbb{Z}_{p},\mathbb{Z}_{p})
equipped with the étale topology. Then the natural map

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}\mathcal{G}\to\left(\mathbb{B}\mathcal{H}\right)^{h\Gamma}

(2.6.3)

is an equivalence.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of Lemma
2.6.3
and Proposition
A.2.11
.
∎

2.6.5.
Homotopy fixed points of shtukas

There is a natural map
\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G}}\to\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{H}}^{h\Gamma}
of categories fibered in groupoids over
\operatorname{Perf}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
; this follows from Lemma
2.5.2
and Lemma
A.1.9
.

Lemma 2.6.6
.

The natural map

\displaystyle\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G}}\to\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{H}}^{h\Gamma}

(2.6.4)

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

To prove that this is an equivalence, it suffices to do this fiberwise over
\operatorname{Spd}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
; so fix
S\to\operatorname{Spd}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
corresponding to an untilt
S^{\sharp}
of
S
. The category of
\mathcal{H}
-shtukas over
S
can be described as the
2
-fiber product

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{H}}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}\mathcal{H}(S\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\times}\limits^{\boldsymbol{.}}}\mathbb{Z}_{p})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}\mathcal{H}(S\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\times}\limits^{\boldsymbol{.}}}\mathbb{Z}_{p})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}\mathcal{H}(S\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\times}\limits^{\boldsymbol{.}}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\setminus S^{\sharp})\times\mathbb{B}\mathcal{H}(S\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\times}\limits^{\boldsymbol{.}}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{\operatorname{Frob}_{S}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}

(2.6.5)

where
\Delta
is the diagonal and where
\Gamma_{\operatorname{Frob}_{S}}
is the graph of pullback along
\operatorname{Frob}_{S}
. This diagram is
\Gamma
-equivariant because the right vertical and bottom horizontal maps are equivariant by Lemma
A.2.1
. Lemma
A.1.11
tells us that the homotopy fixed points of the diagram give a
2
-Cartesian diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{H}}(S)^{h\Gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}\mathcal{H}(S\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\times}\limits^{\boldsymbol{.}}}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{h\Gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}\mathcal{H}(S\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\times}\limits^{\boldsymbol{.}}}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{h\Gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}\mathcal{H}(S\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\times}\limits^{\boldsymbol{.}}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\setminus S^{\sharp})^{h\Gamma}\times\mathbb{B}\mathcal{H}(S\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\times}\limits^{\boldsymbol{.}}}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{h\Gamma},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{\operatorname{Frob}_{S}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}

(2.6.6)

which after repeatedly applying Corollary
2.6.4
can be identified with the
2
-Cartesian diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{H}}(S)^{h\Gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}\mathcal{G}(S\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\times}\limits^{\boldsymbol{.}}}\mathbb{Z}_{p})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}\mathcal{G}(S\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\times}\limits^{\boldsymbol{.}}}\mathbb{Z}_{p})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}\mathcal{G}(S\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\times}\limits^{\boldsymbol{.}}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\setminus S^{\sharp})\times\mathbb{B}\mathcal{G}(S\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\times}\limits^{\boldsymbol{.}}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{\operatorname{Frob}_{S}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}

(2.6.7)

By construction, the map
\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G}}
to
\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{H}}(S)^{h\Gamma}
compatible is compatible with the
2
-fiber product description, and thus an isomorphism.
∎

2.6.7.

Recall that we also use
\mu
to denote the induced
H(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
-conjugacy class of cocharacters of
H
, and we will later do this for all other groups.

Lemma 2.6.8
.

The natural map

\displaystyle\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G},\mu}\to\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{H},\mu}^{h\Gamma}

(2.6.8)

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
2.6.6
as soon as we can prove that a
\mathcal{G}
-shtuka whose induced
\mathcal{H}
-shtuka is bounded by
\mu
, is itself bounded by
\mu
. This follows from the fact that the natural isomorphism

\displaystyle\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathcal{G}}\to\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\Gamma}

(2.6.9)

induces an isomorphism
\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{G},\mu}^{\mathrm{v}}\to\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{H},\mu}^{\mathrm{v},\Gamma}
, see Proposition
2.4.8
.
∎

Lemma 2.6.9
.

Let
\operatorname{Spec}L\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Q}_{p}
be a finite étale Galois cover (not necessarily unramified) with Galois group
\Gamma
and let
H=\operatorname{Res}_{L/\mathbb{Q}}G_{L}
. Then the natural map

\displaystyle\operatorname{Bun}_{G}\to\operatorname{Bun}_{\mathrm{H}}^{h\Gamma}

(2.6.10)

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

It follows from the proof of Corollary
2.6.4
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}G\to\left(\mathbb{B}H\right)^{h\Gamma}

(2.6.11)

is an equivalence on the category of adic spaces over
\operatorname{Spa}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathbb{Z}_{p})
. Indeed, the point is that we can use Lemma
2.2.1
after the finite étale cover
\operatorname{Spa}(L,\mathcal{O}_{L})\to\operatorname{Spa}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathbb{Z}_{p})
and then conclude using Corollary
A.2.13
.
∎

Now we return to the assumption that
\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{L}\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is a finite étale cover with Galois group
\Gamma
. If
b:\operatorname{Spd}\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}\to\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{H},\mu}
is induced by
b:\operatorname{Spd}\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}\to\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G},\mu}
, then there is an action of
\Gamma
on
\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathcal{H},b,\mu}
.

Proposition 2.6.10
.

The natural map
\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathcal{G},b,\mu}\to\left(\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathcal{H},b,\mu}\right)^{\Gamma}
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

The Cartesian diagram defining
\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathcal{H},b,\mu}
is
\Gamma
-equivariant by Lemma
2.6.1
and the fact that
b
is induced by
b:\operatorname{Spd}\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}\to\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G},\mu}
. The proposition then follows by taking
\Gamma
-homotopy fixed points of this diagram, and using Lemma
A.1.11
in combination with Lemmas
2.6.8
and
2.6.9
.
∎

3.
Fixed points of Shimura varieties

Let
{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}
be a Shimura datum and let
\mathsf{F}
be a totally real Galois extension of
\mathbb{Q}
with Galois group
\Gamma
. Let
H:=\operatorname{Res}_{\mathsf{F}/\mathbb{Q}}G_{F}
equipped with its natural action of
\Gamma
. For
h:\mathbb{S}\to\mathsf{G}_{\mathbb{R}}
in
\mathsf{X}
we let
\mathsf{Y}
be the
\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{R})
-conjugacy class of the composition
h:\mathbb{S}\to G_{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathsf{H}_{\mathbb{R}}
, this does not depend on the choice of
h
. Then
{(\mathsf{H},\mathsf{Y})}
is a Shimura datum and the action of
\Gamma
on
\mathsf{H}
is by automorphisms of Shimura data. Following a suggestion of Rapoport, we call
{(\mathsf{H},\mathsf{Y})}
the
Piatetski-Shapiro construction
for
{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}
associated to
\mathsf{F}
.
5
5

5

This construction was used by Borovoi
[
2
]
and Milne
[
22
]
in their proof of the conjecture of Langlands on conjugation of Shimura varieties, and they credit it to Piatetski-Shapiro.
The natural closed immersion
\mathsf{G}\to\mathsf{H}
defines a morphism of Shimura data. The goal of this section is to investigate the fixed points of the action of
\Gamma
on the Shimura varieties for
{(\mathsf{H},\mathsf{Y})}
, and to compare them to the Shimura varieties for
{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}
.

3.1.
The case of tori

As a toy example, we study the case when
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{T}
is a torus with
\mathbb{R}
-split rank zero. Then the
\mathbb{R}
-split rank of

\mathsf{H}=\operatorname{Res}_{\mathsf{F}/\mathbb{Q}}\mathsf{T}_{F}
is also zero, and thus for neat
K\subset\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
the natural map

\displaystyle\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{Q})\to\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{A}_{f})/K

(3.1.1)

is injective by Lemma
3.2.1
. The cokernel of this injection defines the set of
\mathbb{C}
-points of the Shimura variety
\mathbf{Sh}_{K}{(\mathsf{H},\mathsf{Y})}
. If
K
is
\Gamma
-stable, then
\Gamma
acts on
\mathbf{Sh}_{K}{(\mathsf{H},\mathsf{Y})}
and there is a long exact sequence of abelian groups

\displaystyle 0\to K^{\Gamma}\to\mathsf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})\to\left(\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{A}_{f})/K)\right)^{\Gamma}\to\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,K)\to\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{A}_{f}))\to\cdots

(3.1.2)

We will see in Proposition
3.4.7
that it is possible to find arbitrary small
\Gamma
-stable
K
with
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,K)=0
, at least if
\mathsf{F}
is tamely ramified over
\mathbb{Q}
. So from now on we will assume that
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,K)=0
, which implies that the natural map

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{f})/K^{\Gamma}\to\left(\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{A}_{f})/K)\right)^{\Gamma}.

(3.1.3)

is an isomorphism and that the natural map

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{A}_{f})/K)\to\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{A}_{f}))

(3.1.4)

is injective. It moreover implies that there is a long exact sequence

\displaystyle 0\to\mathsf{G}(\mathbb{Q})\to\mathsf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{f})/K^{\Gamma}\to\mathbf{Sh}_{K}{(\mathsf{H},\mathsf{Y})}^{\Gamma}(\mathbb{C})\to\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{Q}))\to\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{A}_{f})/K).

(3.1.5)

Thus the obstruction to the natural map

\displaystyle\mathbf{Sh}_{K^{\Gamma}}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}(\mathbb{C})\to\mathbf{Sh}_{K}{(\mathsf{H},\mathsf{Y})}^{\Gamma}(\mathbb{C})

(3.1.6)

being a bijection is given by the kernel of

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{Q}))\to\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{A}_{f})).

(3.1.7)

We will later prove, see Lemma
3.3.1
, that this can be identified with the kernel of

\displaystyle\Sha^{1}(\mathbb{Q},\mathsf{G})\to\Sha^{1}(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{G}),

(3.1.8)

in particular, the obstruction is finite. In the rest of the section, we will prove that the same result, suitably interpreted, holds for arbitrary Shimura varieties.

3.2.
Shimura varieties and Shimura stacks

Let the notation be as in the start of Section
3
and let
K\subset\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
be a compact open subgroup. It turns out that it is easier to analyze the
\Gamma
-homotopy fixed points of the stack quotient/action groupoid

\displaystyle\left[\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash\left(\mathsf{Y}\times\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})/K\right)\right],

(3.2.1)

than it is to analyze the
\Gamma
-fixed points of the Shimura variety
\mathbf{Sh}_{K}{(\mathsf{H},\mathsf{Y})}
with
\mathbb{C}
-points

\displaystyle\mathbf{Sh}_{K}{(\mathsf{H},\mathsf{Y})}(\mathbb{C})=\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash\left(\mathsf{Y}\times\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})/K\right).

(3.2.2)

Thus it is important for us to understand when the Shimura variety for
{(\mathsf{H},\mathsf{Y})}
is equal to the stack quotient in (
3.2.1
) (for sufficiently small
K
).

Recall Milne’s axiom SV5 for a Shimura datum
{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}
from
[
23
, p. 64]
; it asks that
Z(\mathbb{Q})\subset Z(\mathbb{A}_{f})
is discrete, where
Z=Z_{\mathsf{G}}
is the center of
\mathsf{G}
. By
[
15
, Lemma 1.5.5]
this happens if and only if the
\mathbb{Q}
-split rank of
Z
is equal to the
\mathbb{R}
-split rank of
Z
. The following lemma is well known, see
[
23
, Proposition 3.1, Lemma 5.13]
.

Lemma 3.2.1
.

If axiom SV5 holds for
{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}
, then for neat
K
the group
\mathsf{G}(\mathbb{Q})
acts freely on
\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})/K
.

Unfortunately if SV5 holds for
{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}
then it often does not hold for
{(\mathsf{H},\mathsf{Y})}
. For example if
Z_{\mathsf{G}}=\mathbb{G}_{m}
, then
Z_{\mathsf{H}}=\operatorname{Res}_{\mathsf{F}/\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{G}_{m}
, which has
\mathbb{Q}
-split rank one but
\mathbb{R}
-split rank equal to
[\mathsf{F}:\mathbb{Q}]
. We make this observation precise in the following lemma.

Lemma 3.2.2
.

Suppose that
{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}
satisfies SV5 and that
[\mathsf{F}:\mathbb{Q}]>1
. Then
{(\mathsf{H},\mathsf{Y})}
satisfies SV5 if and only if the
\mathbb{R}
-split rank of
Z_{\mathsf{G}}
is zero.

Proof.

By
[
15
, Lemma 1.5.5]
, there is an isogeny
Z_{\mathsf{G}}\sim\mathsf{T}_{1}\times\mathsf{T}_{2}
where
\mathsf{T}_{1}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-split and
\mathsf{T}_{2}
has
\mathbb{R}
-rank zero. This induces an isogeny

\displaystyle Z_{\mathsf{H}}\simeq\operatorname{Res}_{\mathsf{F}/\mathbb{Q}}\mathsf{T}_{1,\mathsf{F}}\times\operatorname{Res}_{\mathsf{F}/\mathbb{Q}}\mathsf{T}_{2,\mathsf{F}}.

(3.2.3)

The torus
\operatorname{Res}_{\mathsf{F}/\mathbb{Q}}\mathsf{T}_{2,\mathsf{F}}
still has
\mathbb{R}
-rank zero. The torus
\operatorname{Res}_{\mathsf{F}/\mathbb{Q}}\mathsf{T}_{1,\mathsf{F}}
has
\mathbb{Q}
-rank equal to the
\mathbb{Q}
-split rank
d
of
Z_{\mathsf{G}}
, and has
\mathbb{R}
-split rank equal to
d\cdot[\mathsf{F}:\mathbb{Q}]
. Thus the
\mathbb{Q}
-split rank of
Z_{\mathsf{H}}
is equal to the
\mathbb{R}
-split rank of
Z_{\mathsf{H}}
if and only if
d=0
.
∎

3.3.
Fixed points of adelic quotients

Let the notation be as in the start of Section
3
. Before we investigate the homotopy fixed points of the groupoid (
3.2.1
), we first investigate the fixed points of
\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash\mathsf{Y}\times\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
. To understand the
\Gamma
-fixed points of the quotient
\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{A})
, we need to understand the map
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{Q}))\to\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{A}))
. For this we will use the inflation maps

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{Q}))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,\mathsf{G}(\mathsf{F}))\to\mathrm{H}^{1}(\operatorname{Gal}_{\mathbb{Q}},\mathsf{G}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}))=:\mathrm{H}^{1}(\mathbb{Q},\mathsf{G})

(3.3.1)

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{A}))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,\mathsf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{\mathsf{F}}))\to\mathrm{H}^{1}(\operatorname{Gal}_{\mathbb{Q}},\mathsf{G}(\overline{\mathbb{A}})),

(3.3.2)

where
\overline{\mathbb{A}}=\mathbb{A}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
and
\mathbb{A}_{\mathsf{F}}=\mathbb{A}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathsf{F}
. As explained on
[
26
, p. 298]
, there is an injective map

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{1}(\operatorname{Gal}_{\mathbb{Q}},\mathsf{G}(\overline{\mathbb{A}}))\to\prod_{v}\mathrm{H}^{1}(\operatorname{Gal}_{\mathbb{Q}_{v}},\mathsf{G}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{v})),

(3.3.3)

such that the induced map
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\operatorname{Gal}_{\mathbb{Q}},\mathsf{G}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}))\to\prod_{v}\mathrm{H}^{1}(\operatorname{Gal}_{\mathbb{Q}_{v}},\mathsf{G}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}))
is the natural map.

Lemma 3.3.1
.

The inflation map induces a bijection

\displaystyle\ker\left(\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{Q}))\to\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{A}))\right)\to\ker\left(\Sha^{1}(\mathbb{Q},\mathsf{G})\to\Sha^{1}(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{G})\right).

(3.3.4)

Proof.

The inflation map
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{Q}))\to\mathrm{H}^{1}(\mathbb{Q},\mathsf{G})
is injective by
[
31
, Section 5.8.(a)]
and lands
\Sha^{1}(\mathbb{Q},\mathsf{G})
by the discussion above, and so we are done.
∎

Since
\mathsf{F}
is totally real, it follows that
\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{R})=\operatorname{Hom}(\Gamma,\mathsf{G}(\mathbb{R}))
and therefore by Lemma
2.2.1
that
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{R}))=\{1\}
. Thus we observe that

\displaystyle\ker\left(\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{Q}))\to\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{A}_{f}))\right)=\ker\left(\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{Q}))\to\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{A}))\right).

(3.3.5)

Lemma 3.3.2
.

If
\Sha^{1}(\mathbb{Q},\mathsf{G})\to\Sha^{1}(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{G})
is injective, then the natural map

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{f})\to\left(\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash\mathsf{Y}\times\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{A}_{f})\right)^{\Gamma}

(3.3.6)

is a bijection.

Proof.

By
[
31
, Corollary 1 on page 50]
, there is a short exact sequence of pointed sets

\displaystyle 1\to\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{Q})^{\Gamma}\to\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{A})^{\Gamma}\to\left(\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{A})\right)^{\Gamma}\to\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{Q}))\to\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{A}))\to\cdots

(3.3.7)

It moreover follows from loc. cit. that if
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{Q}))\to\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{A}))
has trivial kernel then
\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{A})^{\Gamma}\to\left(\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{A})\right)^{\Gamma}
is surjective. Thus by Lemma
3.3.1
and our assumption, the natural map

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash\mathsf{G}(\mathbb{A})\to\left(\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{A})\right)^{\Gamma}

(3.3.8)

is a bijection. Let
\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{X}}\subset\mathsf{G}(\mathbb{R})
be the stabilizer of some point
x\in\mathsf{X}
and let
\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{Y}}
be its stabilizer inside of
\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{R})
. Since
\mathsf{F}
is totally real it follows that
\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{R})=\operatorname{Hom}(\Gamma,\mathsf{G}(\mathbb{R}))
and also that
\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{Y}}=\operatorname{Hom}(\Gamma,\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{X}})
; thus
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{Y}})=\{1\}
and
\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{X}}=\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{Y}}^{\Gamma}
.

We can identify the natural map of the lemma with

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash\mathsf{G}(\mathbb{A})/\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{X}}\to\left(\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{A})/\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{Y}}\right)^{\Gamma}.

(3.3.9)

The lemma now follows from the fact that
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{Y}})=\{1\}
in combination with Corollary
A.2.13
.
∎

3.4.
Constructing good compact open subgroups

In order to apply Lemma
3.3.2
to Shimura varieties (or stacks), we need to understand the
\Gamma
-cohomology of compact open subgroups of
\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
. In this section, we are going to show that there exist many compact open subgroups with vanishing
\Gamma
-cohomology, at least if
\mathsf{F}
is tamely ramified over
\mathbb{Q}
.

Let
p
be a prime number and write
\mathcal{O}_{F}:=\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{F}}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. It is a finite flat algebra over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
equipped with an action of
\Gamma
. If
\mathcal{G}
is a smooth affine group scheme over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
with generic fiber isomorphic to
G:=\mathsf{G}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, we define
\mathcal{H}:=\operatorname{Res}_{\mathcal{O}_{F}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{O}_{F}}
. It is a smooth affine group scheme over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
with generic fiber isomorphic to
\operatorname{Res}_{F/\mathbb{Q}_{p}}G_{F}
, where
F=\mathsf{F}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. Moreover the Galois group
\Gamma
acts on
\mathcal{H}
.

Lemma 3.4.1
.

As
\mathcal{G}
runs over all smooth affine group schemes over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
with connected special fiber and with generic fiber isomorphic to
G
, the groups
\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
form a cofinal collection of compact open subgroups of
H(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
.

Proof.

By the results
[
12
, Remark A.5.14, Lemma A.5.15]
we can construct a sequence of smooth group schemes
\mathcal{G}_{1}\leftarrow\mathcal{G}_{2}\leftarrow\mathcal{G}_{3}\leftarrow\cdots
over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, such that: The induced maps
\mathcal{G}_{i+1,\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\to\mathcal{G}_{i,\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
are isomorphisms, there is an isomorphism
\mathcal{G}_{1,\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\simeq G
and by
[
12
]
*Lemma A.5.13 we have

\bigcap_{i=1}^{\infty}(\operatorname{Res}_{\mathcal{O}_{F}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{G}_{i,\mathcal{O}_{F}})(\mathbb{Z}_{p})=\{1\}.

The lemma follows by replacing each
\mathcal{G}_{i}
with the open subgroup whose special fiber is the identity component of
\mathcal{G}_{i,\mathbb{F}_{p}}
.
∎

We now show that if
p
is unramified in
\mathsf{F}
, then the cohomology of
\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
is trivial.

Lemma 3.4.2
.

If
p
is unramified in
\mathsf{F}
, then for each smooth affine group scheme
\mathcal{G}
over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
with connected special fiber and with generic fiber isomorphic to
G
, the group
\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})=\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{O}_{F})
satisfies

\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}))=\{1\}.

Proof.

Choose a prime
\mathfrak{p}
of
\mathsf{F}
above
p
and let
\Gamma^{\prime}\subset\Gamma
be its stabilizer. Then there is a
\Gamma
-equivariant isomorphism of rings

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{F}\simeq\operatorname{Map}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(\Gamma,\mathcal{O}_{F,\mathfrak{p}}),

(3.4.1)

which induces a
\Gamma
-equivariant isomorphism of groups

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{O}_{F})

(3.4.2)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Map}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(\Gamma,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{O}_{F,\mathfrak{p}})).

(3.4.3)

By Lemma
2.2.1
, it then suffices to show that

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma^{\prime},\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{O}_{F,\mathfrak{p}}))=\{1\}.

(3.4.4)

Consider the inflation map (which is injective by
[
31
, Section 5.8.(a)]
)

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma^{\prime},\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{O}_{F,\mathfrak{p}}))\to\mathrm{H}^{1}(\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{ur}}/\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{ur}})).

(3.4.5)

Because
\mathcal{H}
is smooth with connected special fiber, Lang’s lemma tells us that

\mathrm{H}^{1}(\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{ur}}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}),\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{ur}}))=\mathrm{H}^{1}(\operatorname{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}/\mathbb{F}_{p}),\mathcal{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}))=\{1\}

and so we are done.
∎

If
p
is coprime to the order of
\Gamma
, then the cohomology of
\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
is trivial if it is a pro-
p
group.

Lemma 3.4.3
.

Let
\Gamma
be a finite group acting on a finite
p
-group
M
. If the order of
\Gamma
is coprime to
p
, then
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,M)=\{1\}
.

Proof.

We prove this by strong induction on
k
where
p^{k}
is the order of
M
. If
k=1
then the result is true because then
M\simeq\mathbb{F}_{p}
is abelian and
\Gamma
is a group of order prime-to-
p
.

To prove the induction step, we use the well-known fact that the center
Z\subset M
of
M
is nontrivial because
M
is a
p
-group. Since
\Gamma
acts via group homomorphisms, we see that
Z
is
\Gamma
-stable. We now study the long exact sequence in
\Gamma
-cohomology for

\displaystyle 1\to Z\to M\to M/Z\to 1.

(3.4.6)

The induction hypothesis tells us that
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,M/Z)=\{1\}
and because
Z
is abelian and of
p
-power order it follows that
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,Z)=\{1\}
. The induction step then follows from the long exact sequence in
\Gamma
-cohomology for (
3.4.6
).
∎

Lemma 3.4.4
.

If
p
is coprime to the order of
\Gamma
and
\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
is pro-
p
, then
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}))=\{1\}
.

Proof.

There is a
\Gamma
-equivariant identification
\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})=\varprojlim_{n}\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z})
. Let
\sigma:\Gamma\to\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
be a cocycle and let
P
be the (possibly empty) set of elements
h\in\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
such that for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
we have
1=h\cdot\sigma(\gamma)\cdot\alpha_{\gamma}(\mathrm{H}^{-1})
, where
\alpha_{\gamma}:\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\to\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
denotes the action of
\gamma
. Then
\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\Gamma}
acts on
P
by left multiplication on
h
and this action is simply transitive if
P
is nonempty. The lemma is asserting that
P
is nonempty, which we will now prove.

For a positive integer
n
, we let
P_{n}
be the set of elements
h_{n}\in\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z})
such that for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
we have
1=h_{n}\cdot\sigma_{n}(\gamma)\cdot\alpha_{\gamma}(h_{n}^{-1})
, where
\sigma_{n}
is the composition of
\sigma
with
\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\to\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z})
. Then
P_{n}
is nonempty by Lemma
3.4.3
and
\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z})^{\Gamma}
acts simply transitively on
P_{n}
by left multiplication on
h_{n}
. There are maps
P_{n+1}\to P_{n}
which are

\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{Z}/p^{n+1}\mathbb{Z})^{\Gamma}
-equivariant via the natural map
\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{Z}/p^{n+1}\mathbb{Z})^{\Gamma}\to\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z})^{\Gamma}
, and it follows from the definitions that the natural map

\displaystyle P\to\varprojlim_{n}P_{n}

(3.4.7)

is a bijection. To show that
P
is nonempty, it is therefore enough to show that the transition maps
P_{n+1}\to P_{n}
are surjective. For this, we consider the
\Gamma
-equivariant short exact sequence

\displaystyle 1\to Q\to\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{Z}/p^{n+1}\mathbb{Z})\to\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z})\to 1

(3.4.8)

defining
Q
. Note that
Q
is again a
p
-group, and thus it follows from Lemma
3.4.3
and the long exact sequence in
\Gamma
-cohomology that
\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{Z}/p^{n+1}\mathbb{Z})^{\Gamma}\to\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z})^{\Gamma}
is surjective. We deduce that
P_{n+1}\to P_{n}
is surjective, which concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 3.4.5
.

Suppose that
p
is tamely ramified in
\mathsf{F}
. Let
\mathcal{G}
be a smooth affine group scheme over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
with connected special fiber and with generic fiber isomorphic to
G
. If
\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})=\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{O}_{F})
is a pro-
p
group then

\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}))=\{1\}.

Proof.

Choose a prime
\mathfrak{p}
of
\mathsf{F}
above
p
and let
L
be the completion of
\mathsf{F}
at
\mathfrak{p}
. Let
\operatorname{Gal}(L/\mathbb{Q}_{p})\subset\Gamma
be the stabilizer of
\mathfrak{p}
. As in the proof of Lemma
3.4.2
, we can use Lemma
2.2.1
to reduce the lemma to proving that
