{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}
, using Theorem
3.5.1
as input on the generic fiber (thus we require the assumption that the
\mathbb{R}
-split rank of
Z_{\mathsf{G}}
is zero). To do this, one needs integral models satisfying the Pappas–Rapoport axioms, see
[
5
]
for this.
10
10

10

In an earlier version of this paper, we proved a weaker version of these axioms for the same integral models of Shimura varieties in order to prove a version of Theorem
5.1.2
. Our weaker version involved constructing shtukas for
\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{ad}}
, which is easier than constructing
\mathcal{G}
-shtukas, see
[
5
, Remark 1.1]
.
In fact, the proof of such a theorem is easier than the proof of Theorem
5.1.2
, because one can directly work with
{(\mathsf{H},\mathsf{Y})}
instead of
{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}
.

Remark 5.1.5
.

If
p
is completely split in
\mathsf{F}
, then the proof of part (1) of Theorem
5.1.2
can be adapted to show that the natural map is actually an isomorphism. The key point is that if
p
is completely split, then one can prove a version of Lemma
2.4.4
for the integral local Shimura varieties for
\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{ad},\circ}
, which then implies that Proposition
2.6.10
holds on the level of formal schemes. We leave the details to the interested reader.

6.
Fixed points of Igusa stacks

In this section we discuss the fixed points of the Igusa stacks of
[
3
]
. We use this to prove Theorem
1
, see Theorem
6.3.1
.

6.1.
Igusa stacks

Fix an isomorphism
\mathbb{C}\to\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
. For
\mathbb{Q}_{p}\subset L\subset\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
let us denote by
\operatorname{ShTrp}_{L,\operatorname{Hdg}}
the category whose objects are triples
{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X},\mathcal{G})}
(with
\mathcal{G}
quasi-parahoric) such that
\mathsf{E}\to\mathbb{C}\to\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
factors through
L
and such that
{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X},\mathcal{G})}
is of Hodge type, and whose morphisms are morphisms
f:{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}\to(\mathsf{G}^{\prime},\mathsf{X}^{\prime})
such that
f
extends (necessarily uniquely) to a morphism
\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. By
[
4
, Theorem I, Corollary 4.1.10]
, there is a functor
\mathscr{S}^{\diamond}:\operatorname{ShTrp}_{L,\operatorname{Hdg}}\to\mathcal{D}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{O}_{L}}
sending
{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X},\mathcal{G})}
to
\mathscr{S}_{K_{p}}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}^{\diamond}
, see Section
4.2.1
. Here
K_{p}=\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
and
\mathscr{S}_{K_{p}}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}^{\diamond}
is the diamond associated to the formal scheme
\widehat{\mathscr{S}}_{K_{p}}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}
, which is the
p
-adic completion of the integral model
\mathscr{S}_{K_{p}}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}
.

The following result is a consequence of
[
3
, Theorem I, Theorem VII]
as we will explain below. It was originally conjectured by Scholze.

Theorem 6.1.1
.

There is a functor
\operatorname{Igs}:\operatorname{ShTrp}_{L,\operatorname{Hdg}}\to\mathcal{D}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{O}_{L}}
together with a natural transformation
\mathscr{S}^{\diamond}\to\operatorname{Igs}
and a weak natural transformations
\operatorname{Igs}\to\operatorname{Bun}
, and a strictly commutative diagram of weak natural transformations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{S}^{\diamond}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sht}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Igs}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Bun},}

(6.1.1)

such that the resulting
2
-commutative diagrams of stacks on
\operatorname{Perf}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{S}_{K_{p}}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}^{\diamond}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sht}_{\mathcal{G},\mu,\delta=1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Igs}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Bun}_{G},}

(6.1.2)

are
2
-Cartesian for all
{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X},\mathcal{G})}\in\operatorname{ShTrp}_{L,\operatorname{Hdg}}
, and such that
\operatorname{Igs}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}\to\operatorname{Bun}_{G}
factors through the open substack
\operatorname{Bun}_{G,\mu^{-1}}\to\operatorname{Bun}_{G}
.

Proof.

The existence of
\operatorname{Igs}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}
is
[
3
, Theorem VII, Theorem 6.0.1, Remark 6.0.2]
, the existence of the strict functor
\operatorname{Igs}
and the strict natural transformation
\mathscr{S}^{\diamond}\to\operatorname{Igs}
is
[
3
, Theorem I]
. The weak natural transformation
\operatorname{Igs}\to\operatorname{Bun}
is constructed at the end of the proof of
[
3
, Proposition 7.1.6]
, see the commutative cube in
[
3
, Equation (7.1.4)]
. By construction of the Igusa stack, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{S}_{K_{p}}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}^{\diamond}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G},\mu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Igs}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Bun}_{G},}

(6.1.3)

is strictly commutative for all
{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X},\mathcal{G})}
, and thus we see that the result follows.
∎

6.2.
Fixed points of Igusa stacks

Let the notation be as in Section
5.1
.

Theorem 6.2.1
.

If
\Sha^{1}(\mathbb{Q},\mathsf{G})\to\Sha^{1}(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{G})
is injective, then the natural map

\displaystyle\operatorname{Igs}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}\to\operatorname{Igs}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}^{\Gamma}\times_{\operatorname{Bun}_{H_{1}}^{h\Gamma}}\operatorname{Bun}_{G,\mu^{-1}}

(6.2.1)

is an isomorphism.

To prove the theorem, we need to introduce potentially crystalline loci.

6.2.2.
The potentially crystalline locus in Hodge type Shimura varieties

Recall that there is an open immersion
\mathbf{Sh}_{K}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}^{\circ,\mathrm{an}}\subset\mathbf{Sh}_{K}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}^{\mathrm{an}}
of rigid spaces over
E
called the
potentially crystalline locus
, constructed in
[
11
]
, see
[
11
, Theorem 5.17]
. The formation of
\mathbf{Sh}_{K}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}^{\circ,\lozenge}
is compatible with changing
K
, see
[
11
, Corollary 5.29]
, and we will also consider

\displaystyle\mathbf{Sh}_{K_{p}}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}^{\circ,\lozenge}

\displaystyle=\varprojlim_{K^{p}\subset\mathsf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})}\mathbf{Sh}_{K^{p}K_{p}}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}^{\circ,\lozenge}

(6.2.2)

\displaystyle\mathbf{Sh}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}^{\circ,\lozenge}

\displaystyle=\varprojlim_{K\subset\mathsf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})}\mathbf{Sh}_{K}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}^{\circ,\lozenge}.

(6.2.3)

Lemma 6.2.3
.

If
{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}\to(\mathsf{G}^{\prime},\mathsf{X}^{\prime})
is a closed immersion of Shimura data of Hodge type, then for
K_{p}^{\prime}\subset G^{\prime}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
containing
K_{p}\subset G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
there are equalities of open subdiamonds

\displaystyle\mathbf{Sh}_{K_{p}}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}^{\circ,\lozenge}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Sh}_{K_{p}^{\prime}}(\mathsf{G}^{\prime},\mathsf{X}^{\prime})^{\circ,\lozenge}\times_{\mathbf{Sh}_{K_{p}^{\prime}}(\mathsf{G}^{\prime},\mathsf{X}^{\prime})^{\lozenge}}\mathbf{Sh}_{K_{p}}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}^{\lozenge}

(6.2.4)

\displaystyle\mathbf{Sh}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}^{\circ,\lozenge}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Sh}(\mathsf{G}^{\prime},\mathsf{X}^{\prime})^{\circ,\lozenge}\times_{\mathbf{Sh}(\mathsf{G}^{\prime},\mathsf{X}^{\prime})^{\lozenge}}\mathbf{Sh}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}^{\lozenge}.

(6.2.5)

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of
[
11
, Lemma 2.2]
, cf.
[
3
, Lemma 5.1.6, Lemma 5.1.7]
.
∎

The following lemma is
[
3
, Lemma 5.1.6]
.

Lemma 6.2.4
.

There is an equality

\displaystyle\mathbf{Sh}_{K}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}^{\circ,\lozenge}=\mathscr{S}_{K_{p}}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}^{\diamond}\times_{\operatorname{Spd}\mathcal{O}_{E}}\operatorname{Spd}E

(6.2.6)

of open subdiamonds of
\mathbf{Sh}_{K}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}^{\lozenge}
.

Let
K_{p,1}^{\circ}\subset K_{p}\cap H_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
be the unique parahoric subgroup, corresponding to the identity component
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\circ}
of
\mathcal{H}_{1}
.

Lemma 6.2.5
.

If
\Sha^{1}(\mathbb{Q},\mathsf{G})\to\Sha^{1}(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{G})
is injective, then the following diagram is
2
-Cartesian

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Sh}_{K_{p}^{\circ,\Gamma}}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}^{\circ,\lozenge}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Sh}_{K_{p,1}^{\circ}}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}^{\circ,\Gamma,\lozenge}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G},\mu,E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\circ},\mu,E}\right)^{h\Gamma}.}

(6.2.7)

Proof.

It follows from Lemma
3.6.7
that the natural map (where the limit runs over
\Gamma
-stable compact open subgroups of
\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
)

\displaystyle\varprojlim_{K\subset\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})}\mathbf{Sh}_{K^{\Gamma}}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}^{\lozenge}\to\varprojlim_{K\subset\mathsf{H}(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})}\mathbf{Sh}_{K_{1}}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}^{\lozenge,\Gamma}

(6.2.8)

is an isomorphism. We then invoke Lemma
6.2.4
to get an isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathbf{Sh}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}^{\circ,\lozenge}\to\mathbf{Sh}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}^{\circ,\lozenge}.

(6.2.9)

The map
\mathbf{Sh}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}^{\circ,\lozenge}\to\mathbf{Sh}_{K_{p,1}^{\circ}}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}^{\circ,\lozenge}
is a torsor for the sheaf of groups
\underline{K_{p,1}^{\circ}}
associated to the topological group
K_{p,1}^{\circ}
. Now we observe that there is a natural (in particular
\Gamma
-equivariant) isomorphism

\displaystyle\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\circ},\mu,E}\simeq\left[\operatorname{Gr}_{H,\mu^{-1}}/\underline{K_{p,1}^{\circ}}\right],

(6.2.10)

see
[
38
, Proposition 11.17]
. Moreover, by construction, the
\underline{K_{p,1}^{\circ}}
-torsor over
\mathbf{Sh}_{K_{p,1}^{\circ}}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}^{\circ,\lozenge}
coming from the map

\displaystyle\mathbf{Sh}_{K_{p,1}}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}^{\circ,\lozenge}\to\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\circ},\mu,E},

(6.2.11)

is given by
\mathbf{Sh}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}^{\circ,\lozenge}\to\mathbf{Sh}_{K_{p,1}^{\circ}}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}^{\circ,\lozenge}
. Thus we have a
\Gamma
-equivariant
2
-Cartesian diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Sh}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}^{\circ,\lozenge}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Gr}_{H,\mu^{-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Sh}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}^{\circ,\lozenge}/\underline{K_{p,1}^{\circ}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\operatorname{Gr}_{H,\mu^{-1}}/\underline{K_{p,1}^{\circ}}\right],}

(6.2.12)

whose homotopy fixed points are again
2
-Cartesian by Lemma
A.1.11
. The homotopy fixed points diagram looks like (see the proof of Proposition
2.4.8
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Sh}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}^{\circ,\lozenge}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Gr}_{G,\mu^{-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\mathbf{Sh}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}^{\circ,\lozenge}/\underline{K_{p,1}^{\circ}}\right)^{\Gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\operatorname{Gr}_{H,\mu^{-1}}/\underline{K_{p,1}^{\circ}}\right]^{h\Gamma}.}

(6.2.13)

If we base change the bottom row via
\mathbb{B}\underline{K_{p}^{\circ,\Gamma}}\to\mathbb{B}\underline{K_{p,1}^{\circ}}
, then by Proposition
A.2.7
we get the Cartesian diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Sh}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}^{\circ,\lozenge}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Gr}_{G,\mu^{-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Sh}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}^{\circ,\lozenge}/\underline{K_{p}^{\circ,\Gamma}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\operatorname{Gr}_{G,\mu^{-1}}/\underline{K_{p}^{\circ,\Gamma}}\right].}

(6.2.14)

By Proposition
A.2.7
, this proves that the following diagram is Cartesian

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Sh}_{K_{p}^{\circ,\Gamma}}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}^{\circ,\lozenge}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Sh}_{K_{p,1}^{\circ}}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}^{\circ,\Gamma,\lozenge}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\operatorname{Gr}_{G,\mu^{-1}}/\underline{K_{p}^{\circ,\Gamma}}\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\operatorname{Gr}_{H,\mu^{-1}}/\underline{K_{p,1}^{\circ}}\right]^{h\Gamma},}

(6.2.15)

proving the lemma.
∎

Proof of Theorem
6.2.1
.

It follows from Theorem
6.1.1
that there is a
2
-commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Igs}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Bun}_{G,\mu^{-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Igs}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}^{h\Gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Bun}_{H_{1}}^{h\Gamma}.}

(6.2.16)

This induces a map

\displaystyle\operatorname{Igs}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}\to\operatorname{Igs}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}^{h\Gamma}\times_{\operatorname{Bun}_{H_{1}}^{h\Gamma}}\operatorname{Bun}_{G,\mu^{-1}}

(6.2.17)

which we will show is an isomorphism. By v-descent we can do this after basechanging via the v-cover

\displaystyle\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G}^{\circ},\mu,E}\to\operatorname{Bun}_{G,\mu^{-1}}

(6.2.18)

from
[
3
, Corollary 6.4.2]
. Using Theorem
6.1.1
, Lemma
6.2.4
and Lemma
A.1.11
, we can identify the basechanged map with the natural map

\displaystyle\mathbf{Sh}_{K_{p}^{\circ,\Gamma}}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}^{\circ,\lozenge}\to\mathbf{Sh}_{K_{p,1}^{\circ}}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}^{\circ,\lozenge,\Gamma}\times_{\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\circ},\mu,E}^{h\Gamma}}\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G}^{\circ},\mu,E}.

(6.2.19)

This natural map is an isomorphism by Lemma
6.2.5
.
∎

6.3.
Fixed points of integral models of Shimura varieties of Hodge type II

We now prove Theorem
1
, which we will restate for the convenience of the reader. Let
{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}
be a Shimura datum of Hodge type with reflex field
\mathsf{E}
. Fix a prime
p
and a prime
v
of
\mathsf{E}
above
p
, let
E,G,\mathcal{O}_{E},\mathsf{F},\Gamma,F,\mathcal{O}_{F}
be as above. Let
\mathcal{G}
be a parahoric model of
G
over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, let
\mathcal{H}=\operatorname{Res}_{\mathcal{O}_{F}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{O}_{F}}
and let
K_{p}=\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
. Let
\mathcal{H}_{1}
be the group smoothening of the Zariski closure of
\mathcal{H}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})\cap H_{1}(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
in
\mathcal{H}
; this is a quasi-parahoric model of
H_{1}
with
\mathcal{H}_{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})=K_{p,1}=K_{p}\cap H_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
.

Theorem 6.3.1
.

If
\Sha^{1}(\mathbb{Q},\mathsf{G})\to\Sha^{1}(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{G})
is injective, then the following diagram of v-stacks is
2
-Cartesian

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{S}_{K_{p}^{\circ,\Gamma}}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}^{\lozenge/}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{S}_{K_{p,1}}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}^{\Gamma,\lozenge/}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G}^{\circ},\mu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{H}_{1},\mu}^{h\Gamma}.}

(6.3.1)

Proof.

If we basechange the isomorphism

\displaystyle\operatorname{Igs}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}\to\operatorname{Igs}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}^{h\Gamma}\times_{\operatorname{Bun}_{H_{1}}^{h\Gamma}}\operatorname{Bun}_{G,\mu^{-1}}

(6.3.2)

of Theorem
6.2.1
via
\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G}^{\circ},\mu}\to\operatorname{Bun}_{G,\mu^{-1}}
, then by Theorem
6.1.1
we get the natural map

\displaystyle\mathscr{S}_{K_{p}^{\circ,\Gamma}}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}^{\diamond}\to\mathscr{S}_{K_{p,1}^{\circ}}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}^{\Gamma,\diamond}\times_{\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{H}_{1},\mu}^{h\Gamma}}\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G}^{\circ},\mu},

(6.3.3)

which is therefore an isomorphism of v-sheaves. If we combine this isomorphism with Lemma
6.2.5
(or rather its generalization to quasi-parahoric subgroups, using
\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{H}_{1},\mu,\delta=1}
and
[
4
, Corollary 3.3.9]
), then we see that the natural map

\displaystyle\mathscr{S}_{K_{p}^{\circ,\Gamma}}{(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{X})}^{\lozenge/}\to\mathscr{S}_{K_{p,1}^{\circ}}{(\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{1})}^{\Gamma,\lozenge/}\times_{\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{H}_{1},\mu}^{h\Gamma}}\operatorname{Sht}_{\mathcal{G}^{\circ},\mu},

(6.3.4)

is an isomorphism (this follows from the definition of
\lozenge/
, see Section
2.3.3
). This concludes the proof.
∎

Appendix A
Some
(2,1)
-category theory

A.1.
Strict
(2,1)
-categories and weak functors

Recall from
[
32
, Section 02X8]
the definition of a strict
(2,1)
-category. We will use
\operatorname{Mor}^{1}(x,y)
to refer to the category of
1
-morphisms between
x
and
y
in such a category, and sometimes abusively to refer to the class of objects of this category using the same notation. We will sometimes refer to isomorphisms in the category
\operatorname{Mor}^{1}(x,y)
as natural transformations.

Definition A.1.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a 1-category and
\mathcal{D}
a strict
(2,1)
-category. We define a
weak functor

\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
to be a pair
(\mathcal{F},\eta_{\mathcal{F}})
consisting of:

(1)

An assignment

\mathcal{F}:\operatorname{Ob}(\mathcal{C})\to\operatorname{Ob}(\mathcal{D})

and a map
\mathcal{F}:\operatorname{Mor}(x,y)\to\operatorname{Ob}(\operatorname{Mor}^{1}(\mathcal{F}(x),\mathcal{F}(y)))
.

(2)

For every
x\in\operatorname{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
a 2-morphism
\eta_{\mathcal{F},x}:\text{Id}_{\mathcal{F}(x)}\to\mathcal{F}(\text{Id}_{x})
.

(3)

For every composable pair
f:x\to y
,
g:y\to z
in
\mathcal{C}
a 2-morphism
\eta_{\mathcal{F},f,g}:\mathcal{F}(g\circ f)\to\mathcal{F}(g)\circ\mathcal{F}(f)
.

Such that:

(1)

For any morphism
f:x\to y
in
\mathcal{C}
we have that

\eta_{\mathcal{F},f,\text{Id}_{y}}=\eta_{\mathcal{F},y}\circ\text{Id}_{\mathcal{F}(f)}

and

\eta_{\mathcal{F},\text{Id}_{x},f}=\text{Id}_{\mathcal{F}(f)}\circ\eta_{\mathcal{F},x}.

(2)

For any composable triple
f:x\to y,g:y\to z,h:z\to t
we have

(\text{Id}_{\mathcal{F}(h)}\circ\eta_{\mathcal{F},f,g})\circ\eta_{\mathcal{F},g\circ f,h}=(\eta_{\mathcal{F},g,h}\circ\text{Id}_{\mathcal{F}(f)})\circ\eta_{\mathcal{F},f,h\circ g}.

Definition A.1.2
.

A weak natural transformation
\epsilon:\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{G}
of weak functors
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is:

(1)

A collection for
x\in\operatorname{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
of
\epsilon_{x}:\mathcal{F}(x)\to\mathcal{G}(x)
of 1-morphisms in
\mathcal{D}
.

(2)

For each morphism
f:x\to y
in
\mathcal{C}
, a natural transformation
\epsilon(f):\epsilon_{y}\circ\mathcal{F}(f)\to\mathcal{G}(f)\circ\epsilon_{x}
in the category
\operatorname{Mor}^{1}(\mathcal{F}(x),\mathcal{G}(y))
, we will denote this in diagram form by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(y),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(f)}

satisfying the following conditions:

(1)

We have an equality of natural transformations for every pair of morphisms
f:x\to y,g:y\to z
:

\epsilon(g\circ f)\circ\eta_{\mathcal{F},f,g}=\eta_{\mathcal{G},f,g}\circ\mathcal{G}(g)_{*}(\epsilon(f))\circ\mathcal{F}(f)^{*}(\epsilon(g)).

(2)

For every object
x\in\operatorname{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
we ask for commutativity of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{x}\circ\text{Id}_{\mathcal{F}(x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Id}_{\mathcal{G}(x)}\circ\epsilon_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{x}\circ\mathcal{F}(\text{Id}_{x})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(\text{Id}_{x})\circ\epsilon_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{\mathcal{F},x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{\mathcal{G},x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon(\text{Id}_{x})}

inside of the category
\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{F}(x),\mathcal{G}(x))
. We note that because we work with strict
(2,1)
-categories the commutativity of this diagram is well-posed.

Let
\Gamma
be an abstract group. We define the category
B\Gamma
to be the classifying category of the abstract group
\Gamma
(that is, the category with one object
\ast
with automorphism group
\Gamma
). Suppose
\mathcal{D}
is a strict
(2,1)
-category.

Definition A.1.3
.

A
\Gamma
-object in
\mathcal{D}
is a weak functor
\mathcal{F}:B\Gamma\to\mathcal{D}
. A
\Gamma
-equivariant morphism is a weak natural transformation of such functors.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a strict
(2,1)
-category and let
x
be an object of
\mathcal{D}
. Then a weak functor
\mathcal{F}:B\Gamma\to\mathcal{D}
sending
\ast
to
x
is called a
weak
\Gamma
-action on
x
.

Example A.1.4
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a strict
(2,1)
-category and let
x
be an object of
\mathcal{D}
. Then the trivial
\Gamma
-action on
x
is the following weak functor
\mathcal{F}:B\Gamma\to\mathcal{D}
: On objects it sends
\ast
to
x
and on morphisms it sends all morphisms to the identity
x\to x
. Moreover the
2
-morphisms
\eta_{\mathcal{F},x}
and
\eta_{\mathcal{F},f,g}
are all taken to be the identity
2
-morphism.

Example A.1.5
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a strict
(2,1)
-category and let
x
be an object of
\mathcal{D}
such that
\operatorname{Mor}^{1}(x,x)
is a discrete category.
11
11

11

A discrete category is a category where the only morphisms are the identity morphisms.
Then
\operatorname{Ob}(\operatorname{Mor}^{1}(x,x))
is a group under composition of morphisms and a weak
\Gamma
-action on
x
is the same as a group homomorphism
\Gamma\to\operatorname{Ob}(\operatorname{Mor}^{1}(x,x))
.

A.1.6.

Recall
[
32
, Tag 003R]
that fiber products in the
(2,1)
-category of categories have the following description. Let
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}
be
(2,1)
-categories and let
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{C},\mathcal{G}:\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{C}
be functors. Then there is the following canonical presentation of the fiber product:

Definition A.1.7
.

The fiber product
\mathcal{A}\times_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{B}
identifies with the strict
(2,1)
-category whose objects consist of triples
(a,b,f)
where
a\in\operatorname{Ob}(\mathcal{A}),b\in\operatorname{Ob}(\mathcal{B})
and
f:\mathcal{F}(a)\to\mathcal{G}(b)
is an isomorphism in
\mathcal{C}
. The morphisms
\phi:(a,b,f)\to(c,d,g)
consist of pairs
(X,Y)
where
X:a\to c,Y:b\to d
are such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(c)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(d)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

commutes.

Definition A.1.8
.

Let
X
be a
\Gamma
-object in the strict
(2,1)
-category of categories, defined a weak functor
\mathcal{F}
. Then we define the
\Gamma
-
homotopy fixed points

X^{h\Gamma}
of
X
to be the following category: The objects of
X^{h\Gamma}
are tuples
(x,\{\tau_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma})
, where
x\in\operatorname{Ob}(X)
is an object and
\tau_{\gamma}:x\to\mathcal{F}(\gamma)(x)
for each
\gamma\in\Gamma
is an isomorphism such that for all
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}
the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(\gamma^{\prime})(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(\gamma^{\prime}\gamma)(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(\gamma^{\prime})(\mathcal{F}(\gamma)(x))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{\gamma^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{\gamma^{\prime}\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(\gamma^{\prime})(\tau_{\gamma})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{\mathcal{F},\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}}

is commutative. A morphism
f:(x,\tau)\to(x^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
of such objects is a morphism
f:x\to x^{\prime}
such that the following diagram commutes for all
\gamma\in\Gamma

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(\gamma)(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(\gamma)(x^{\prime}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{\prime}_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(f)}

(A.1.1)

Lemma A.1.9
.

A
\Gamma
-equivariant morphism
\alpha:X\to Y
of
\Gamma
-objects in the strict
(2,1)
-category of categories defines a natural functor

\displaystyle\alpha^{h\Gamma}:X^{h\Gamma}\to Y^{h\Gamma}.

(A.1.2)

Furthermore, this functor is an equivalence if
\alpha
is an equivalence.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{CAT}
be the strict
(2,1)
-category of categories and suppose that
X
and
Y
are given by weak functors
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}:B\Gamma\to\mathcal{CAT}
. Then the morphism
\alpha
is precisely a weak natural transformation
\epsilon:\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{G}
. In particular, for each
\gamma\in\Gamma
we have a natural transformation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(\gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(\gamma)}

(A.1.3)

Given a tuple
(x,\{\tau_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma})\in X^{h\Gamma}
we would like to define its image under
\alpha^{h\Gamma}
to be the tuple
(\alpha(x),\{\epsilon_{\gamma}\circ\alpha(\tau_{\gamma})\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma})
. To check that this tuple defines an object of
Y^{h\Gamma}
, we need to check that for
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma
the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(\gamma^{\prime})(\alpha(x))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(\gamma^{\prime}\gamma)(\alpha(x))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(\gamma^{\prime})(\mathcal{G}(\gamma)(\alpha(x))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{\gamma^{\prime}}\circ\alpha(\tau_{\gamma^{\prime}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{\gamma^{\prime}\gamma}\circ\alpha(\tau_{\gamma^{\prime}\gamma})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(\gamma^{\prime})(\epsilon_{\gamma}\circ\alpha(\tau_{\gamma}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{\mathcal{G},\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}}

(A.1.4)

commutes. But this is a direct consequence of the fact that
\epsilon
is a weak natural transformation. Thus we can define
\alpha^{h\Gamma}
on the level of objects by sending

(x,\{\tau_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma})\in X^{h\Gamma}
to the tuple
(\alpha(x),\{\epsilon_{\gamma}\circ\alpha(\tau_{\gamma})\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma})\in Y^{h\Gamma}
.

Given a morphism
f:(x,\{\tau_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma})\to(x^{\prime},\{\tau^{\prime}_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma})
in
X^{h\Gamma}
, one can check that

\displaystyle\alpha(f):\alpha(x)\to\alpha(x^{\prime})

(A.1.5)

is a morphism in
Y^{h\Gamma}
. The association
f\mapsto\alpha(f)
is compatible with compositions, since
\alpha
is a functor from
X
to
Y
. Thus we have constructed the desired functor
\alpha^{h\Gamma}
.

If
\alpha
is fully faithful, then it is immediate that
\alpha^{h\Gamma}
is also fully faithful. If
\alpha
is essentially surjective and fully faithful, then
\alpha^{h\Gamma}
is also essentially surjective. Indeed, given
(y,\{\kappa_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma})\in Y^{h\Gamma}
we may choose an object
x\in X
and an isomorphism
\xi:\alpha(x)\to y
. For each
\gamma\in\Gamma
there is an induced isomorphism

\displaystyle\alpha(\mathcal{F}(\gamma))\to\mathcal{G}(\gamma)(y)

(A.1.6)

given by
\mathcal{G}(\gamma)(\xi)\circ\epsilon_{\gamma}
. Using the fully faithfulness, the isomorphism
\kappa_{\gamma}:y\to\mathcal{G}(\gamma)(y)
induces a unique isomorphism
\tau_{\gamma}:x\to\alpha(x)
. Using this uniqueness, it is not hard to check that the tuple
(x,\{\tau_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma})
defines an object in
X^{h\Gamma}
. Its image under
\alpha^{h\Gamma}
is moreover isomorphic to
(y,\{\kappa_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma})
by construction.
∎

Lemma A.1.10
.

Let
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{CAT}
be weak functors, where
\mathcal{C}
is a 1-category and
\mathcal{CAT}
is the strict
(2,1)
-category of categories. Let
\delta:\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{H},\epsilon:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
be weak natural transformations. Then there exists a canonical weak functor
\mathcal{F}\times_{\mathcal{H}}\mathcal{G}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{CAT}
such that
(\mathcal{F}\times_{\mathcal{H}}\mathcal{G})(x)=\mathcal{F}(x)\times_{\mathcal{H}(x)}\mathcal{G}(x)
for all
x\in\operatorname{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

Step 1: Objects.
We define
\mathcal{P}:=(\mathcal{F}\times_{\mathcal{H}}\mathcal{G})
on objects by sending
x\mapsto\mathcal{F}(x)\times_{\mathcal{H}(x)}\mathcal{G}(x)
. For
x,y\in\mathcal{C}
we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}:\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}(x,y)\to\operatorname{Ob}\left(\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{F}(x)\times_{\mathcal{H}(x)}\mathcal{G}(x),\mathcal{F}(y)\times_{\mathcal{H}(y)}\mathcal{G}(y)\right)

(A.1.7)

to be the assignment taking
g:x\to y
to the functor

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(g)\times_{\mathcal{H}(g)}\mathcal{G}(g):\mathcal{F}(x)\times_{\mathcal{H}(x)}\mathcal{G}(x)\to\mathcal{F}(y)\times_{\mathcal{H}(y)}\mathcal{G}(y)

(A.1.8)

taking
(a,b,f)\in\mathcal{F}(x)\times_{\mathcal{H}(x)}\mathcal{G}(x)
to the triple
(\mathcal{F}(g)(a),\mathcal{G}(g)(b),\mathcal{P}(g)(f))
, where
\mathcal{P}(g)(f))
is defined as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{y}(\mathcal{F}(g)(a))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(g)(\delta_{x}(a))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(g)(\delta_{x}(b))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{y}(\mathcal{G}(g)(b)).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}(g)(f))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta(g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(g)(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon(g)^{-1}}

(A.1.9)

Step 2: Morphisms.
A morphism
p:(a,b,f)\to(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},f^{\prime})
is sent to the morphism

\displaystyle(\mathcal{F}(g)(p),\mathcal{G}(g)(p))

(A.1.10)

which one checks is a morphism in
\mathcal{F}(y)\times_{\mathcal{H}(y)}\mathcal{G}(y)
. To check that
\mathcal{F}(g)\times_{\mathcal{H}(g)}\mathcal{G}(g)
is a functor, one uses the fact that
\delta
and
\epsilon
are weak natural transformations (and thus have compatibility with composition).

Step 3: Identity natural transformations.
Next, for each
x\in\operatorname{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
we define a natural transformation
\eta_{x}:\text{Id}_{\mathcal{P}(x)}\to(\mathcal{P})(\text{Id}_{x})
given by the morphism

\displaystyle\eta_{x,(a,b,f)}:(a,b,f)\to(\mathcal{F}(\operatorname{Id}_{x})(a),\mathcal{G}(\operatorname{Id}_{x})(b),\mathcal{P}(\operatorname{Id}_{x})(f))

(A.1.11)

described by
(\eta_{\mathcal{F},x,a},\eta_{\mathcal{G},x,a})
. Naturality follows from the naturality of
\eta_{\mathcal{F}}
and
\eta_{\mathcal{G}}
, and we must simply check that the morphisms
(\eta_{\mathcal{F},x,a},\eta_{\mathcal{G},x,b})
are morphisms in the category
\mathcal{F}(x)\times_{\mathcal{H}(x)}\mathcal{G}(x)
. We recall that this comes down to showing that the top square in the following diagram is commutative for every
(a,b,f)\in\operatorname{Ob}(\mathcal{F}(x)\times_{\mathcal{H}(x)}\mathcal{G}(x))

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{x}(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{x}(b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{x}(\mathcal{F}(\text{Id}_{x})(a))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{x}(\mathcal{G}(\text{Id}_{x})(b))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(\text{Id}_{x})(\delta_{x}(a))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(\text{Id}_{x})(\epsilon_{x}(b)).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{x}(\eta_{\mathcal{F},x,a})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{x}(\eta_{\mathcal{G},x,b})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta(\text{Id}_{x})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}(\operatorname{Id}_{x})(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon(\text{Id}_{x})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(\text{Id}_{x})(f)}

(A.1.12)

But we know that the bottom square is commutative by the definition of
\mathcal{P}(\operatorname{Id}_{x})(f)
. Moreover, because
\delta
is a weak natural transformation from
\mathcal{F}
to
\mathcal{H}
, we know that
\delta(\text{Id}_{x})\circ\delta_{x}(\eta_{\mathcal{F},x,a})=\eta_{\mathcal{H},x}\circ\delta_{x}
as morphisms between
\delta_{x}(a)
and
\mathcal{H}(\text{Id}_{x})(\delta_{x}(a))
in the category
\mathcal{H}(x)
. In addition
\epsilon(\text{Id}_{x})\circ\epsilon_{x}(\eta_{\mathcal{G},x,b})=\eta_{\mathcal{H},x,b}\circ\epsilon_{x}
as morphisms from
\epsilon_{x}(b)
to
\mathcal{H}(\text{Id}_{x})(\epsilon_{x}(b))
. The commutativity of the outer square of (
A.1.12
) follows from these last two observations. Since we also have commutativity of the bottom diagram, the commutativity of the top diagram follows since the vertical arrows are all isomorphisms.

Step 4: Natural transformations for compositions of morphisms.
Finally, for a pair of morphisms
\gamma^{\prime}:x\to y
and
\gamma:y\to z\in\mathcal{C}
we define a natural transformation
\eta_{\mathcal{P},\gamma^{\prime},\gamma}:\mathcal{P}(\gamma^{\prime}\circ\gamma)\to\mathcal{P}(\gamma^{\prime})\circ\mathcal{P}(\gamma)
of functors
\mathcal{P}(x)\to\mathcal{P}(z)
which is defined on objects
(a,b,f)\in\mathcal{P}(x)
by

(\eta_{\mathcal{F},\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}(a),\eta_{\mathcal{G},\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}(b)).

Once again, the naturality is straightforward if we can check that this is an isomorphism in
\mathcal{P}(z)
. For this, we consider the following diagram of isomorphisms in
\mathcal{H}(z)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(\gamma^{\prime}\circ\gamma)(\delta_{x}(a))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(\gamma^{\prime}\circ\gamma)(\epsilon_{x}(a))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{z}(\mathcal{F}(\gamma^{\prime}\circ\gamma)(a))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{z}(\mathcal{G}(\gamma^{\prime}\circ\gamma)(b))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{z}(\mathcal{F}(\gamma^{\prime})(\mathcal{F}(\gamma)(a)))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{z}(\mathcal{G}(\gamma^{\prime})(\mathcal{G}(\gamma)(b)))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(\gamma^{\prime})(\delta_{y}(\mathcal{F}(\gamma)(a)))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(\gamma^{\prime})(\epsilon_{y}(\mathcal{G}(\gamma)(a)))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(\gamma^{\prime})(\mathcal{H}(\gamma)(\delta_{x}(a)))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(\gamma^{\prime})(\mathcal{H}(\gamma)(\epsilon_{x}(b))).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(\gamma^{\prime}\circ\gamma)(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon(\gamma^{\prime}\gamma)^{-1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta(\gamma^{\prime}\gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{z}(\eta_{\mathcal{F},\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}(a))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}(\gamma^{\prime}\circ\gamma)(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{z}(\eta_{\mathcal{G},\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}(b))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta(\gamma^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}(\gamma^{\prime})(\mathcal{P}(\gamma)(f))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(\gamma^{\prime})(\mathcal{P}(\gamma)(f))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(\gamma^{\prime})(\delta(\gamma))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon(\gamma^{\prime})^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(\gamma^{\prime})(\mathcal{H}(\gamma)(f))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{H}(\gamma^{\prime})(\epsilon(\gamma))^{-1}}

(A.1.13)

We are asked to show that the second square from the top commutes, and we see that this reduces to checking commutativity of the outer square in the diagram. But this again follows from the fact that
\delta
and
\epsilon
are weak natural transformations.

Step 5: End of the proof.
We have now specified the data required to define a weak functor
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{CAT}
. To check that this is a weak functor, we need to check certain identities of natural transformations hold, see Definition
A.1.1
. Since all natural transformations
\eta_{\mathcal{P}}
are defined as pairs
(\eta_{\mathcal{F}},\eta_{\mathcal{G}})
, the identities for
\eta_{\mathcal{P}}
follow from those for
\eta_{\mathcal{F}}
and
\eta_{\mathcal{G}}
.
∎

We have the following key lemma. Let
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H}:B\Gamma\to\mathcal{CAT}
be weak functors and let
\delta:\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{H},\epsilon:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
be weak natural transformations. Recall the fiber product weak functor
\mathcal{F}\times_{\mathcal{H}}\mathcal{G}
from Lemma
A.1.10
.

Lemma A.1.11
.

There is an equivalence of categories

\beta:\mathcal{F}^{h\Gamma}(\ast)\times_{\mathcal{H}^{h\Gamma}(\ast)}\mathcal{G}^{h\Gamma}(\ast)\to(\mathcal{F}\times_{\mathcal{H}}\mathcal{G})(\ast)^{h\Gamma}.

Proof.

Let us write
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{F}\times_{\mathcal{H}}\mathcal{G}
for simplicity.

Step 1: Defining the functor on objects.
The functor
\beta
has the following construction on objects: Let
(a,\{\tau_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma})
be an object in
\mathcal{F}^{h\Gamma}(\ast)
, let
(b,\{\sigma_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma})
be an object in
\mathcal{G}^{h\Gamma}(\ast)
and let
f:\delta^{h\Gamma}(a,\{\tau_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma})\to\epsilon^{h\Gamma}(b,\{\sigma_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma})
be an isomorphism in
\mathcal{H}^{h\Gamma}(\ast)
. Observe that
f
is per definition an automorphism
\delta(a)\to\epsilon(b)
in
\mathcal{H}
satisfying certain extra properties.

We define
\beta((a,\{\tau_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma}),(b,\{\sigma_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma}),f)
to be the triple
(a,b,f,\{\rho_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma})
, where
\rho_{\gamma}
is the pair
(\tau_{\gamma},\rho_{\gamma})
. To check that our triple defines an element of
\mathcal{P}(\ast)^{h\Gamma}
, we need to check that for all
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}
the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(a,b,f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}(\gamma^{\prime})(a,b,f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}(\gamma^{\prime}\gamma)(a,b,f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}(\gamma^{\prime})(\mathcal{P}(\gamma)(a,b,f))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{\gamma^{\prime}\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}(\gamma)(\rho_{\gamma})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{\mathcal{P},\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}}

(A.1.14)

commutes. But morphisms in
\mathcal{P}(\ast)^{h\Gamma}
are determined by the corresponding morphisms in
\mathcal{P}(\ast)
, which are in turn determined by a pair consisting of a morphism in
\mathcal{F}(\ast)
and a morphism in
\mathcal{G}(\ast)
. The commutativity of the diagram (
A.1.14
) then follows from the definition of
\eta_{\mathcal{P},\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}
and
\mathcal{P}
, in combination with the fact that
(a,\{\tau_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma})
is an object in
\mathcal{F}^{h\Gamma}(\ast)
and the fact that
(b,\{\sigma_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma})
is an object of
\mathcal{G}^{h\Gamma}(\ast)
.

Step 2: Defining the functor on morphisms.
A morphism

g:((a,\{\tau_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma}),(b,\{\sigma_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma}),f)\to((a^{\prime},\{\tau^{\prime}_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma}),(b^{\prime},\{\sigma^{\prime}_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma}),f^{\prime})

in
\mathcal{F}^{h\Gamma}(\ast)\times_{\mathcal{H}^{h\Gamma}(\ast)}\mathcal{G}^{h\Gamma}(\ast)
corresponds to a pair of morphisms
(g_{1},g_{2})
. Concretely, we have
g_{1}:a\to a^{\prime}
and
g_{2}:b\to b^{\prime}
such that the following diagrams commute for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(\gamma)(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(\gamma)(a^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{\prime}_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(g_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(\gamma)(b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(\gamma)(b^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{\prime}_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(g_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon(b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta(a^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon(b^{\prime}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta(g_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon(g_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}

(A.1.15)

We will define
\beta(g)
to be the morphism

\displaystyle(a,b,f,\{\rho_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma})\to(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},f^{\prime},\{\rho^{\prime}_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma})

(A.1.16)

corresponding to
(g_{1},g_{2})
. This is a morphism in
\mathcal{P}(\ast)
between
(a,b,f)
and
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},f)
by the commutativity of the third diagram in (
A.1.15
). To show that this is a morphism in
\mathcal{P}(\ast)^{h\Gamma}
, we have to show that for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(a,b,f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},f^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(\gamma)(a,b,f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(\gamma)(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},f^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{\prime}_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}(\gamma)(g)}

(A.1.17)

commutes. But morphisms in
\mathcal{P}(\ast)
are determined by pairs of morphisms in
\mathcal{F}(\ast)
and
\mathcal{H}(\ast)
. The commutativity now follows from the commutativity of the first two diagrams of (
A.1.15
) and the definition of
P(\gamma)
and
\rho_{\gamma}
.

Step 3: Showing
\beta
is fully faithful.
Suppose we are given objects

((a,\{\tau_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma}),(b,\{\sigma_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma}),f),((a^{\prime},\{\tau^{\prime}_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma}),(b^{\prime},\{\sigma^{\prime}_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma}),f^{\prime})\in\mathcal{F}^{h\Gamma}(\ast)\times_{\mathcal{H}^{h\Gamma}(\ast)}\mathcal{G}^{h\Gamma}(\ast).

We have seen in Step 2 that a morphism between these objects consists of a pair of morphisms
g_{1}:a\to a^{\prime}
and
g_{2}:b\to b^{\prime}
such that the diagrams in (
A.1.15
) commute. There are also conditions for the pair
(g_{1},g_{2})
to define a morphism between

\displaystyle\beta((a,\{\tau_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma}),(b,\{\sigma_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma}),f)),\beta((a^{\prime},\{\tau^{\prime}_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma}),(b^{\prime},\{\sigma^{\prime}_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma}),f^{\prime})),

(A.1.18)

namely the commutativity of the third diagram in (
A.1.15
) and the commutativity of (
A.1.17
). But it is immediate that the latter condition is equivalent to the commutativity of the first two diagrams in (
A.1.15
), proving fully faithfulness.

Step 4: Essential surjectivity of
\beta
.
Let
(a,b,f,\{\rho_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma})
be an object of
\mathcal{P}(\ast)^{h\Gamma}
. Then for each
\gamma\in\Gamma
the map
\rho_{\gamma}
consists of a pair of an isomorphism
\tau_{\gamma}:a\to\mathcal{F}(\gamma)(a)
and an isomorphism
\sigma_{\gamma}:b\to\mathcal{G}(\gamma)(b)
. It is straightforward to show that
(a,b,f,\{\rho_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma})
is equal to (!!) the image under
\beta
of the triple
(a,\{\tau_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma}),(b,\{\sigma_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma}),f)
.
∎

A.2.
Quotient stacks and fixed points

In this section we will study the homotopy fixed points of quotients stacks and in particular the interaction between the formation of quotient stacks and homotopy fixed points.

Let
S
be a small category with all fiber products, equipped with a Grothendieck pretopology. We will consider the strict
(2,1)
-category
\operatorname{Cat}_{/S}
of categories fibered in groupoids over
S
, see
[
32
, Definition 02XS]
. The
2
-fiber product of categories fibered in groupoids as described in
[
32
, Lemma 0040]
is the same as the one described in Definition
A.1.7
, thus Lemma
A.1.11
holds for categories fibered in groupoids.

Let
G
be a sheaf of groups on
S
and consider the category fibered in groupoids
\mathbb{B}G\to S
: Its objects are pairs
(\mathcal{P},T)
where
T
is an object of
S
and where
\mathcal{P}\to T
is a left
G
-torsor. A morphism
(\mathcal{P},T)\to(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},T^{\prime})
is a pair of morphisms
a:T\to T^{\prime}
and
b:\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
such that
b
is
G
-equivariant and such that the following diagram commutes and is Cartesian

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\prime}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}

(A.2.1)

The composition of morphisms is given by concatenating Cartesian diagrams.

Recall the notation of a stack in groupoids over
S
, see
[
32
, Definition 02ZI]
. We observe that the proof of
[
32
, Lemma 04UK]
can be repeated to show that
\mathbb{B}G\to S
is a stack in groupoids over
S
.

Given a morphism
f:G\to G^{\prime}
of sheaves of groups, there is a functor
\mathbb{B}f:\mathbb{B}G\to\mathbb{B}G^{\prime}
sending

\displaystyle(T,\mathcal{P})\mapsto(T,\mathcal{P}\times^{G}G^{\prime})

(A.2.2)

and on morphisms sending
b:\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
to the morphism
b^{\prime}:\mathcal{P}\times^{G}G^{\prime}\to\mathcal{P}\times^{G}G^{\prime}
defined by
b^{\prime}(p,g^{\prime})=(b(p),g^{\prime})
. Here we consider
\mathcal{P}\times^{G}G^{\prime}
as the quotient (sheaf) of
P\times G^{\prime}
by the right action of
G
given by
(p,h)\cdot g=(g^{-1}\cdot p,hf(g))
. We let
G^{\prime}
act on
P\times G^{\prime}
by
g^{\prime}\cdot(p,h)=(p,g^{\prime}h)
, which clearly descends to the quotient
\mathcal{P}\times^{G}G^{\prime}
turning that quotient into a left
G^{\prime}
-torsor.

Lemma A.2.1
.

Let
\operatorname{Grp}_{S}
be the
1
-category of sheaves of groups in
S
. There is a weak functor

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}:\operatorname{Grp}_{S}\to\operatorname{Cat}_{/S}

(A.2.3)

which on objects sends
G
to
\mathbb{B}G
and on morphisms sends
f:G\to G^{\prime}
to
\mathbb{B}f:\mathbb{B}G\to\mathbb{B}G^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Part (1) of Definition
A.1.1
has been specified by the lemma. For part (2) we have to specify a
2
-morphism

\displaystyle\eta_{\mathbb{B},G}:\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbb{B}G}\to\mathbb{B}(\operatorname{Id}_{G}).

(A.2.4)

We take the one which is given on objects by the map

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{P}\times^{G}G

(A.2.5)

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle\mapsto(p,1).

(A.2.6)

Moreover, given
f_{1}:G_{1}\to G_{2}
and
f_{2}:G_{2}\to G_{3}
we have to specify a
2
-morphism

\displaystyle\eta_{\mathbb{B},f_{1},f_{2}}:\mathbb{B}(f_{2}\circ f_{1})\to\mathbb{B}(f_{2})\circ\mathbb{B}(f_{1}).

(A.2.7)

We take the one given by the isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}\times^{G_{1}}G_{3}

\displaystyle\to\left(\mathcal{P}\times^{G_{1}}G_{2}\right)\times^{G_{2}}G_{3}

(A.2.8)

\displaystyle(p,g_{3})

\displaystyle\mapsto((p,1),g_{3}).

(A.2.9)

It is straightforward, if somewhat tedious, to check that these coherence data satisfy Definition
A.1.1

∎

Now let
X
be a sheaf on
S
equipped with a left action of a sheaf of groups
G
. Then we define a category fibered in groupoids
\left[X/G\right]\to S
whose objects are triples
(T,\mathcal{P},\omega)
, where
T
is an object of
S
, where
\mathcal{P}\to T
is a
G
-torsor and where
\omega:\mathcal{P}\to X
is a
G
-equivariant map of sheaves. A morphism
(T,\mathcal{P},\omega)\to(T^{\prime},\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})
is a pair
(a,b)
, where
a:T\to T^{\prime}
is a morphism in
S
and
b:\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is a
G
-equivariant morphism such that the following two diagrams commute

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\prime},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Id}_{X}}

(A.2.10)

and such that the first diagram is Cartesian. We observe that
\left[X/G\right]
is a stack in groupoids over
S
; indeed, the proof of
[
32
, Lemma 0370]
goes through verbatim.

Note that there is a natural forgetful morphism
\left[X/G\right]\to\mathbb{B}G
of categories fibered in groupoids over
S
which sends
(T,\mathcal{P},\omega)\mapsto(T,\mathcal{P})
.

A.2.2.

Let
\operatorname{GrpShv}_{S}
be the category whose objects are triples
(G,X,A)
, where
G
is a sheaf of groups on
S
, where
X
is a sheaf on
S
, and where
A:G\times X\to X
is a left action of
G
on
X
. A morphism
(G,X,A)\to(G^{\prime},X^{\prime},A^{\prime})
consists of a homomorphism of groups
h:G\to G^{\prime}
and a morphism of sheaves
f:X\to X^{\prime}
, such that
f
is
G
-equivariant via
h
. There is an obvious forgetful functor
\operatorname{GrpShv}_{S}\to\operatorname{Grp}_{S}
sending
(G,X,A)
to
G
. We consider
\mathbb{B}
as a weak functor on
\operatorname{GrpShv}_{S}
via this forgetful functor.

Lemma A.2.3
.

There is a weak functor
\mathscr{Q}:\operatorname{GrpShv}_{S}\to\operatorname{Cat}_{/S}
, which on objects sends
(G,X,A)
to
\left[X/G\right]
. There is moreover a weak natural transformation
\mathscr{Q}\to\mathbb{B}
, which on objects induces the natural forgetful functor

\displaystyle\left[X/G\right]\to\mathbb{B}G.

(A.2.11)

Proof.

Given a morphism
(h,f):(G,X,A)\to(G^{\prime},X^{\prime},A^{\prime})
we define a morphism

\displaystyle\mathscr{Q}(h,f):\left[X/G\right]\to\left[X^{\prime}/G^{\prime}\right]

(A.2.12)

by sending
(T,\mathcal{P},\omega)\mapsto(T,\mathcal{P}\times^{G}G^{\prime},\omega_{h})
, where

\displaystyle\omega_{h}:\mathcal{P}\times^{G}G^{\prime}

\displaystyle\to X^{\prime}

(A.2.13)

\displaystyle(p,g^{\prime})

\displaystyle\mapsto A^{\prime}(g^{\prime},f(\omega(p)).

(A.2.14)

For the identity natural transformation

\displaystyle\eta_{\mathscr{Q},(G,X,A)}:\operatorname{Id}_{\mathscr{Q}(G,X,A)}\to\mathscr{Q}(\operatorname{Id}_{G},\operatorname{Id}_{X})

(A.2.15)

we take the isomorphism
(T,\mathcal{P},\omega)\mapsto(T,\mathcal{P}\times^{G}G,\omega_{\operatorname{Id}_{G}})
given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{P}\times^{G}G

(A.2.16)

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle\mapsto(p,1),

(A.2.17)

which is compatible with
\omega
and
\omega_{\operatorname{Id}_{G}}
since

\displaystyle(\omega_{\operatorname{Id}_{G}})(p,1)=A(1,\operatorname{Id}_{X}(\omega(p))=\omega(p).

(A.2.18)

Given a pair of composable morphisms
(h,f):(G_{1},X_{1},A_{1})\to(G_{2},X_{2},A_{2})
and
(h^{\prime},f^{\prime}):(G_{2},X_{2},A_{2})\to(G_{3},X_{3},A_{3})
, we need to specify a natural transformation

\displaystyle\eta_{\mathscr{Q},(h,f),(h^{\prime},f^{\prime})}:\mathscr{Q}(h^{\prime}\circ h,f^{\prime}\circ f)\to\mathscr{Q}(h^{\prime},f^{\prime})\circ\mathscr{Q}(h,f).

(A.2.19)

For this we take the isomorphism in
\left[X_{3}/G_{3}\right]
given by

\displaystyle(S,\mathcal{P}\times^{G_{1}}G_{3},\omega_{h^{\prime}\circ h})\to(S,\left(\mathcal{P}\times^{G_{2}}G_{2}\right)\times^{G_{2}}G_{3}),\left(\omega_{h}\right)_{h^{\prime}})

(A.2.20)

given by the isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}\times^{G_{1}}G_{3}

\displaystyle\to\left(\mathcal{P}\times^{G_{1}}G_{2}\right)\times^{G_{2}}G_{3}

(A.2.21)

\displaystyle(p,g_{3})

\displaystyle\mapsto((p,1),g_{3})

(A.2.22)

from the proof of Lemma
A.2.1
. We have to check that this is an isomorphism in
\left[X_{3}/G_{3}\right]
which comes down to the equality

\displaystyle\omega_{h^{\prime}h}(((p,1),g_{3}))=\omega_{h^{\prime}\circ h}(p,g_{3}).

(A.2.23)

To check this we compute that

\displaystyle\left(\omega_{h}\right)_{h^{\prime}}(((p,1),g_{3}))

\displaystyle=A^{\prime}(g_{3},f^{\prime}(\omega_{h}(p,1)))

(A.2.24)

\displaystyle=A_{3}(g_{3},f^{\prime}(A_{2}(1,f(p))))

(A.2.25)

\displaystyle=A_{3}(g_{3},f^{\prime}(f(p)))

(A.2.26)

\displaystyle=\omega_{h^{\prime}\circ h}(p,g_{3}).

(A.2.27)

As in the proof of Lemma
A.2.1
, we omit the verification that these coherence data satisfy Definition
A.1.1
.

The weak natural transformation
\mathscr{Q}\to\mathbb{B}
is easy to specify because it follows by construction that for
(h,f):(G,X,A)\to(G^{\prime},X^{\prime},A^{\prime})
the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[X/G\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[X^{\prime}/G^{\prime}\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}G^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Q}(h,f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}(h)}

(A.2.28)

is strictly commutative. Thus we can specify the identity natural transformation in (2) of Definition
A.1.2
, which clearly satisfies the properties outlined in that definition.
∎

We will now spell out a particular case of Lemma
A.2.3
: Let
\Gamma
be
an abstract group, let
(G,X,A)\in\operatorname{GrpShv}_{S}
and let
\Gamma\to\operatorname{Isom}_{\operatorname{GrpShv}_{S}}((G,X,A),(G,X,A))
be a group homomorphism. This gives us a functor

\displaystyle B\Gamma\to\operatorname{GrpShv}_{S},

(A.2.29)

which we can compose with the weak functor of Lemma
A.2.3
to get a
\Gamma
-object in categories fibered in groupoids over
S
, see Example
A.1.5
. Concretely, the group homomorphism

\Gamma\to\operatorname{Isom}_{\operatorname{GrpShv}_{S}}((G,X,A),(G,X,A))

comes down to the following data: We have a left action of
\Gamma
on
G
over
S
; let us write
\alpha_{\gamma}:G\to G
for the induced isomorphism of groups for each
\gamma\in\Gamma
. We have a left action of
\Gamma
on
X
, which we denote by
\beta_{\gamma}:X\to X
for each
\gamma\in\Gamma
. These morphisms are subject to the following commutative diagram for all
\gamma\in\Gamma

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\gamma}\times\beta_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}

(A.2.30)

The induced action of
\Gamma
on
\left[X/G\right]
on objects takes a tuple
(T,\mathcal{P},\omega)
and sends it to the tuple
(T,\mathcal{P}\times^{G,\gamma}G,\omega_{\gamma})
. Concretely, we have that

\displaystyle\omega_{\gamma}(p,g)

\displaystyle=A(g,\beta_{\gamma}(\omega(p)))

(A.2.31)

which means that
\omega_{\gamma}(p,1)=\beta_{\gamma}(\omega(p))
.

A.2.4.

By Lemma
A.2.3
there is a
\Gamma
-equivariant morphism

\displaystyle\left[X/G\right]\to\mathbb{B}G

(A.2.32)

and thus by Lemma
A.1.9
there is a functor

\displaystyle\left[X/G\right]^{h\Gamma}\to(\mathbb{B}G)^{h\Gamma}.

(A.2.33)

Here
(\mathbb{B}G)^{h\Gamma}
is the
\Gamma
-homotopy fixed points of
(\mathbb{B}G)
in the sense of definition
A.1.8
, which is a category fibered in groupoids over
S
.

Lemma A.2.5
.

The category fibered in groupoids
(\mathbb{B}G)^{h\Gamma}
is a stack in groupoids over
S
.

Proof.

Descent for morphisms in
(\mathbb{B}G)^{h\Gamma}
follows directly from descent of morphisms in
\mathbb{B}G
since the forgetful map
(\mathbb{B}G)^{h\Gamma}\to\mathbb{B}G
is faithful by construction. Descent for objects in
(\mathbb{B}G)^{h\Gamma}
follows from descent for objects in
\mathbb{B}G
in combination with descent for morphisms in
\mathbb{B}G
, and the weak functoriality of the
\Gamma
action.
∎

A.2.6.

Now let
G^{\Gamma}
be the fixed point sheaf of
G
and let
X^{\Gamma}
be the fixed point sheaf of
X
. Then there is a natural morphism

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}G^{\Gamma}\to(\mathbb{B}G)^{h\Gamma}

(A.2.34)

of categories fibered in groupoids over
S
which sends
(T,\mathcal{P})
to
(T,\mathcal{P}\times^{G^{\Gamma}}G)
equipped with the isomorphisms

\displaystyle\tau_{\gamma,0}:\left(\mathcal{P}\times^{G^{\Gamma}}G\right)

\displaystyle\to\left(\mathcal{P}\times^{G^{\Gamma}}G\right)\times^{G,\gamma}G

(A.2.35)

\displaystyle(p,g)

\displaystyle\mapsto((p,1),\alpha_{\gamma}(g)),

(A.2.36)

and which sends a morphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\prime}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}

(A.2.37)

to the induced diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}\times^{G^{\Gamma}}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\times^{G^{\Gamma}}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\prime},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}

(A.2.38)

where
b_{G}(p,g)=(b(p),g)
, which is well defined by the
G^{\Gamma}
-equivariance of
b
. Similarly there is a natural morphism

\displaystyle\left[X^{\Gamma}/G^{\Gamma}\right]\to\left[X/G\right]^{h\Gamma}

(A.2.39)

of categories fibered in groupoids over
S
which sends
(T,\mathcal{P},\omega)
to
(T,\mathcal{P}\times^{G^{\Gamma}}G,\{\tau_{\gamma,0}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma},\omega_{G})
, where

\displaystyle\omega_{G}:\mathcal{P}\times^{G^{\Gamma}}G\to X

(A.2.40)

is defined by
\omega_{G}(p,g)=A(g,\omega(p))
. By construction, we see that the following diagram of categories fibered in groupoids over
S
is strictly commutative

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[X^{\Gamma}/G^{\Gamma}\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[X/G\right]^{h\Gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{B}G^{\Gamma})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{B}G)^{h\Gamma}.}

(A.2.41)

Proposition A.2.7
.

The diagram in equation (
A.2.41
) is
2
-Cartesian.

We start by proving a lemma.

Lemma A.2.8
.

The natural map
\mathbb{B}G^{\Gamma}\to\left(\mathbb{B}G\right)^{h\Gamma}
is fully faithful.

Proof.

Since this is a morphism of categories fibered in groupoids over
S
, it suffices to prove that the map on fibers is fully faithful. Let
T\in S
, let
\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
be
G^{\Gamma}
-torsors over
T
and let
f:(T,\mathcal{P}\times^{G^{\Gamma}}G,\{\tau_{\gamma,0}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma})\to(T,\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\times^{G^{\Gamma}}G,\{\tau^{\prime}_{\gamma,0}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma})
be an isomorphism in
\left(\mathbb{B}G\right)^{h\Gamma}(T)
.

We need to show that
f
is induced from a unique isomorphism
\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
. If we can show that
f_{2}(p,g)=g
, then
f
is induced from a unique isomorphism
\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
; namely, the unique
G^{\Gamma}
-equivariant morphism sending
p
to
f_{1}(p,1)
. It follows from the definitions of morphisms in
\left(\mathbb{B}G\right)^{h\Gamma}
that for
\gamma\in\Gamma
the following diagram must commute

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\mathcal{P}\times^{G^{\Gamma}}G\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\mathcal{P}\times^{G^{\Gamma}}G\right)\times^{G,\gamma}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\times^{G^{\Gamma}}G\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\times^{G^{\Gamma}}G\right)\times^{G,\gamma}G.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{\gamma,0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{\prime}_{\gamma,0}}

(A.2.42)

Using the definition
\tau_{\gamma,0}(p,g)=(p,1,\gamma(g))
this gives us the equality

\displaystyle((f_{1}(p,g),1),\gamma(f_{2}(p,g))=((f_{1}(p,g),1),\gamma(g)).

(A.2.43)

Thus we see that
f_{2}(p,g)=g
and thus
f
is induced from the unique
G^{\Gamma}
-equivariant map
\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
sending
p\mapsto f_{2}(p,1)
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
A.2.7
.

Both the top horizontal map and the bottom horizontal map are fully faithfull, this follows from the definition and Lemma
A.2.8
. The proposition then comes down to the following claim: Given an object
(T,\mathcal{P},\{\tau_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma},\omega)
of
\left[X/G\right]^{h\Gamma}
such that there is a
G^{\Gamma}
-torsor
\mathcal{Q}\to T
and a
G^{\Gamma}
-equivariant morphism
f:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{P}
inducing an isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}\times^{G^{\Gamma}}G\to\mathcal{Q}

(A.2.44)

under which
\{\tau_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma}
corresponds to
\{\tau_{\gamma,0}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma}
, the composition of
f
with
\omega
factors through
X^{\Gamma}
. Let us write
\omega^{\prime}
for the map
\mathcal{Q}\times^{G^{\Gamma}}G\to X
induced by
f
. In this situation the diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}\times^{G^{\Gamma}}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\mathcal{Q}\times^{G^{\Gamma}}G\right)\times^{G,\gamma}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{\gamma},0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega^{\prime}_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Id}_{X}}

(A.2.45)

commute for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
, which means that for
q\in\mathcal{Q}
we have

\displaystyle\omega^{\prime}(q,1)

\displaystyle=\omega^{\prime}_{\gamma}((q,1),1)=\beta_{\gamma}(\omega^{\prime}(q,1))

(A.2.46)

for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
. We deduce that
\omega^{\prime}
maps
\mathcal{Q}\subset\mathcal{Q}\times^{G^{\Gamma}}G
to
X^{\Gamma}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

A.2.9.

We now study under what conditions the map
\mathbb{B}G^{\Gamma}\to(\mathbb{B}G)^{h\Gamma}
is an equivalence. For this purpose, we introduce cocycles and cohomology groups. Consider the sheaf
\underline{Z}^{1}(\Gamma,G)
whose
T
-points are given by the set of cocycles
Z^{1}(\Gamma,G(T))
with values in
G(T)
. In other words, these are the functions
\sigma:\Gamma\to G(T)
satisfying

\displaystyle\sigma(\gamma\cdot\gamma^{\prime})=\sigma(\gamma)\cdot\alpha_{\gamma}(\sigma(\gamma^{\prime})).

(A.2.47)

There is an action of
G
on
\underline{Z}^{1}(\Gamma,G)
, which takes an element
g\in G(T)
and a cocycle
\sigma\in Z^{1}(\Gamma,G(T))
and sends it to the cocycle

\displaystyle\sigma^{\prime}(\gamma)=g\cdot\sigma(\gamma)\cdot\alpha_{\gamma}(g^{-1}).

(A.2.48)

The quotient set for this action is per definition the first (nonabelian) cohomology group of
\Gamma
with coefficients in
G(T)
, in formulas,

\displaystyle Z^{1}(\Gamma,G(T))/G(T)=\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,G(T)).

(A.2.49)

Given a cocycle
\sigma:\Gamma\to G(T)
and
\gamma\in\Gamma
we get an isomorphism of trivial
G_{T}
-torsors

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\gamma,\sigma}:G_{T}

\displaystyle\to G_{T}\times^{G,\gamma}G

(A.2.50)

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle\mapsto(\alpha_{\gamma^{-1}}(g),\sigma(\gamma)).

(A.2.51)

To check that
(T,G_{T},\{\epsilon_{\gamma,\sigma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma}))
defines an object of
(\mathbb{B}G)^{h\Gamma}
we need to check that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{T}\times^{G,\gamma_{2}}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{T}\times^{G,\gamma_{2}\gamma_{1}}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(G_{T}\times^{G,\gamma_{1}}G\right)\times^{G,\gamma_{2}}G.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{\gamma_{2}\gamma_{1},\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{\gamma_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}(\gamma_{2})(\epsilon_{\gamma_{1},\sigma})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{\mathbb{B},\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}}

(A.2.52)

This comes down to the equality

\displaystyle(\alpha_{\gamma_{2}^{-1}\gamma_{1}}(g),\sigma(\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2})),1)=((\alpha_{\gamma_{2}^{-1}}\alpha_{\gamma_{1}^{-1}}(g),\sigma(\gamma_{1})),\sigma(\gamma_{1}))

(A.2.53)

which follows from the cocycle condition

\displaystyle\sigma(\gamma\cdot\gamma^{\prime})=\sigma(\gamma)\cdot\alpha_{\gamma}(\sigma(\gamma^{\prime})).

(A.2.54)

If we now consider
\underline{Z}^{1}(\Gamma,G)
as a fibered category over
S
, the construction above defines a natural map
\underline{Z}^{1}(\Gamma,G)\to(\mathbb{B}G)^{h\Gamma}
which takes
(T,\sigma\in Z^{1}(\Gamma,G(T))
to
(T,G_{T},\{\epsilon_{\gamma,\sigma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma})
.

Lemma A.2.10
.

This map induces an isomorphism

\displaystyle\left[\underline{Z}^{1}(\Gamma,G)/G\right]\to(\mathbb{B}G)^{h\Gamma}.

(A.2.55)

Proof.

This comes down to showing that the sheaf
\underline{Z}^{1}(\Gamma,G)
represents the functor on
S
sending
T
to the set of
\Gamma
-equivariant structures on the trivial
G
-torsor over
T
. We have seen above how to go from a cocycle
\sigma\in\underline{Z}^{1}(\Gamma,G)(T)
to a
\Gamma
-equivariant structure on the trivial
G
-torsor over
T
. Conversely, let
(\mathcal{P}^{0}\times^{G^{\Gamma}}G,\{\tau_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma})
, be a
\Gamma
-equivariant structure on the trivial
G
-torsor
\mathcal{P}^{0}\times^{G^{\Gamma}}G
where
\mathcal{P}^{0}
is the trivial
G^{\Gamma}
-torsor. Then we get a cocycle by considering the collection of elements
\sigma(\gamma)\in G(T)
defined to be the element of
G(T)
corresponding to the following automorphism of
\mathcal{P}^{0}\times^{G^{\Gamma}}G
over
T

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}^{0}\times^{G^{\Gamma}}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\mathcal{P}^{0}\times^{G^{\Gamma}}G\right)\times^{G,\gamma}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}^{0}\times^{G^{\Gamma}}G.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{\gamma,0}^{-1}}

(A.2.56)

Here
\tau_{\gamma,0}
is the isomorphism

\displaystyle\tau_{\gamma,0}:\left(\mathcal{P}^{0}\times^{G^{\Gamma}}G\right)

\displaystyle\to\left(\mathcal{P}^{0}\times^{G^{\Gamma}}G\right)\times^{G,\gamma}G

(A.2.57)

\displaystyle(p,g)

\displaystyle\mapsto((p,1),\gamma(g))

(A.2.58)

from the beginning of Section
A.2.6
. It is a straightforward yet laborious check that this defines an inverse to the construction above, and the lemma is proved.
∎

We let
\underline{H}^{1}(\Gamma,G)
be the sheafification of the presheaf
T\mapsto\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma,G(T))
on
S
. It follows from Lemma
A.2.10
that there is a natural map
(\mathbb{B}G)^{h\Gamma}\to\underline{H}^{1}(\Gamma,G)
. This fits in a
2
-commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}G^{\Gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{B}G)^{h\Gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{H}^{1}(\Gamma,G),}

(A.2.59)

where the top horizontal map is the natural map, and the bottom horizontal map is the natural inclusion of the cohomology class of the trivial cocycle. We have the following fundamental result.

Proposition A.2.11
.

The diagram in (
A.2.59
) is
2
-Cartesian.

Proof.

The top horizontal arrow is fully faithful by Lemma
A.2.8
. It suffices to show that its essential image consists of those elements of
(\mathbb{B}G)^{\Gamma}
whose image in
\underline{H}^{1}(\Gamma,G)
is trivial. This can be checked locally in the Grothendieck topology on
S
since both source and target are stacks in groupoids over
S
, see Lemma
A.2.5
.

Given an object of
(\mathbb{B}G)^{h\Gamma}
over an object
T
of
S
whose image in
\underline{H}^{1}(\Gamma,G)
is trivial, we replace
T
by a cover to assume that the underlying
G
-torsor is trivial. Then the
\Gamma
-equivariant structure corresponds to a cocycle
\sigma:\Gamma\to G(T)
, and by assumption we may pass to a further cover of
T
to assume that this cocycle is trivial. We now observe that the cocycle being trivial tells us precisely that the
\Gamma
-equivariant structure on the trivial
G
-torsor over
T
is isomorphic to the
trivial

\Gamma
-equivariant structure on the trivial
G
-torsor. In other words, that the corresponding object of
(\mathbb{B}G)^{h\Gamma}(T)
is in the essential image of
(\mathbb{B}G^{h\Gamma})(T)
.
∎

Putting together Proposition
A.2.11
and Proposition
A.2.7
we obtain the following corollaries.

Corollary A.2.12
.

There is a natural
2
-Cartesian diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[X^{\Gamma}/G^{\Gamma}\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[X/G\right]^{\Gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{H}^{1}(\Gamma,G).}

(A.2.60)

Corollary A.2.13
.

If
\underline{H}^{1}(\Gamma,G)
is trivial, then the natural map

\displaystyle\left[X^{\Gamma}/G^{\Gamma}\right]\to\left[X/G\right]^{h\Gamma}

(A.2.61)

is an equivalence.
